JUL 17 1933
FILED
The Honorable
The Secretary of State,
Washington.
Sir:
I wish to inform the Department that the oil
situation in Japan is looming up as a very important
economic problem which may vitally affect her relations
with other countries. There are indications that she
is trying to bring the business of refining and distri-
bution of petroleum products under the control of her
own nationals and possibly even to shift her sources
of supply of crude oil. As an American company* is one
of
* The Socony-Vacuum Corporation.

=== Page 4 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
of the important distributors of gasoline in Japan, and
as her chief source of supply of crude oil in the past
has been the United States, both of these tendencies
have an important bearing on the Japanese-American trade
relations.
Certain steps taken by the Japanese Government
during recent months indicated that attention was being
given to the oil problem and hints have been made that
a change of policy is contemplated. The Minister for
Foreign Affairs, in a conversation with one of my col-
leagues of the diplomatic corps, recently confirmed the
rumor that the authorities are studying the oil question
and he stated that there is a committee working on the
matter.
It is believed that the question is being studied
both by officials of the Depatment of Commerce and
Industry and by the military and naval authorities. The
Japanese oil problem definitely has two phases, the com-
mercial and the military, or naval. The activity noted
at present has to do principally with the commercial
angle, though the matter may have been brought to a crisis
as a result of the international situation.
The basis difficulty confronting the Japanese,from
a military as well as a commercial viewpoint, is the
obvious fact that the oil supplies which are under Japa-
nese control are not adequate for the needs of the country.
The annual crude oil production of Japan Proper and Formosa
during

=== Page 5 === (confidence 0.99)
-3 -
during the last five years has varied from 250,000 to
z00,000* long tons, and, as this oil is of the variety
known as "pocket oil", experts do not believe that there
are possibilities of the supply being increased. The
source of supply next most closely under the control
of Japan is probably that at Fushun where the South
Manchuria Railway extracts oil from the shale refuse
of the Fushun colliery. The Japanese Navy receives all
this oil and it is believed that it has not yet amounted
to more than 50,000 long tons of fuel oil per year. The
cost of production is very high but the colliery is cre-
dited with being able to produce enough shale to give
70,000 tons of fuel oil annually.
The next nearest source of supply, which has been
developed, is North Saghalien. There the Japanese own half
of the fields and the Russians half. For several years the
Japanese company, the North Saghalien Oil Company, has co-
operated with the Russians in operating the latter's wells
and has purchased all of the product. However, a pipe line
hasnow been laid to the western shore of the island and the
Russians are said to be planning to transport their share
of the crude oil to their newly-built refineries in Siberia.
The oil resources of North Saghalien are not known;-at any
rate, to outsiders. It is clear that the Japanese have not
been
* Undisputed figures concerning Japan's production,
importation, and refining of petroleum are almost
impossible of attainment. This is partly due to
the unique manner of classifying oil and oil pro-
ducts in government statistics and partly due to the
fact that much oil is imported in navy tankers and
this does not enter into the published statistics.
For this reason the figures given in this report are
only approximate.

=== Page 6 === (confidence 0.99)
- 4 -
beem working the fields to their capacity, partly for
reasons of policy and partly because of the difficulties
of the climate. Work can be carried on only during four
months of the year. During the 1932 season over 300,000
tons of crude oil were imported into Japan from this
district,of which about one-third were purchased from the
Soviet fields. The North Saghalien Petroleum Company,
which operates the wells in this district, is Japanese-
owned, the largest interest being held by the Nippon
O11 Company. The Japanese Government nominally owns no
shares. However, the Navy has signed a contract to take
all of the output of this company and thereby exercises a
certain amount of control over it.
Therefore, with the exception of the 250,000 tons from
Japan Proper, which finds its way into commerce, and 350,000
tons from Fushun and North Saghalien, which go to the navy,
Japan's oil must come from countries over which she has no
control. Furthermore, she must buy her oil from producing
companies which are controlled by foreigners. In this con-
nection, it should be mentioned parenthetically that for
three years a Japanese group have been trying to operate
some wells on the west coast of Borneo, but due to labor
troubles and sickness the project appears to have met with
little success. Japan's importations of crude oil, inclu-
ding that imported by the Navy, have increased from about
350,000 tons in 1928 to over 600,000 tons in 1932. Her
annual importations of fuel oil have moved up and down
between 700,000 tons and 900,000 tons, and the amount of
gasoline

=== Page 7 === (confidence 0.98)
- 5 -
gasoline imported has increased from 30 million gallons
to more than llo million gallons, during the five-year
poriod.
Although the importations of gasoline and other
petroleum products have nearly quadrupled during the
last five years the domestic refining industry has like-
wise expanded. The gasoline produced in Japan jumped
from 42 million gallons to more than l00 million gallons.
The amount produced from domestic crude did not vary
much during this period, amounting to from 15 to 20 million
gallons. The big increase in gasoline production was the
result of the increase in importations of crude oil from
abroad.
The Japanese petroleum refining industry is almost
entirely controlled by Japanese capital. The only ex-
ception is the fifty per cent interest which the Associated
Oil Company of California has in the Mitsubishi Oil Company.
The Japanese firms import most of the crude oil while
the Socony-Vacuum Corporation(American) and the Rising
Sun Petroleum Company(British, a subsidiary of the Shell)
import practicallv all the gasoline which enters Janan.
The twe foreign compamies are apprehensive lest the duty on
cruke oil of yem twe point twe ninety-five per one hundred gallons
be lowered er abolished altagether.Gasoline is subjeet te a duty ef
yen ten point eighty per one hundred gallons, which already places
the imperters ef gaseline at some disedvantage.
oil to Japan in Japanese tankers. The two foreign com-
panies have in the past been able to hold their own
because

=== Page 8 === (confidence 0.99)
- 6 -
because of their well-developed system of distribution.
They were the first established and they had the capital
to install filling stations and engage in large-scale
advertising. However, they are now facing very serious
difficulties and are being forced to sell at prices
below their costs of production.
The two independent foreign companies state that
in order to better their position they are considering
the investment of more money in Japan, in the form of
refineries. However, they will not do this unless the
Japanese Government declares that it wishes them to do
so and that it will not place further obstacles in their
way. They have recently been trying to obtain a state-
ment from the Government but have not so far succeeded in
getting exactly what they want. In the month of November
1932 the two companies addressed a joint letter to the
Minister of Commerce and Industry asking certain questions,
complete answers to which would virtually constitute a
declaration of future policy on this matter. The reply
which they received was naturally rather evasive and failed
to satisfy them entirely. However, the Chief of the Mining
Bureau later stated verbally to them that the Government
would prefer to see all the refining business done by
Japanese capital and labor, but that if the Japanese
refineries were inadequate to meet the demand then the
Government would prefer to see the foreign companies
refine in Japan rather than import refined products as
at

=== Page 9 === (confidence 0.98)
-7-
at present.
'It is not known to what extent the Chief of the
Mining Bureau was speaking for the Government. What he
said undoubtedly represents the desires of important
sections of the Japanese. However, it is understood
that the military and naval authorities are glad to have
the foreign companies here, with their large storage
facilities. They recently requested the two companies
to supply them with a list of their imports during the
past year and their probable imports in the near future.
Furthermore, there are other difficulties of a financial
nature ahead if the Japanese wish their capital to control
entirely the refining and distribution of petroleum pro-
ducts in Japan. Assuming that they are not yet able, or
ready, to invest their money in petroleum refining and
distribution, the question then arises as to whether
to encourage the foreign companies to establish re-
fineries in Japan. This would secure to them an even
more firm foothold in the market. On the other hand,
if the Japanese Government keeps down the tariff and
other restrictions on marketing the foreign produced
commodity the status quo would be maintained until Japan
has more capital to put into the industry. This is one
of the problems facing the authorities and is probably
an important topic of discussion in the committee to
which the Minister for Foreign Affairs referred(See
second paragraph above).
t

=== Page 10 === (confidence 0.99)
- 8 -
It is well kmown that the Japanese as a nation
have for years wanted to get under the control of their
owm nationals as much of the industry of the nation as
possible. This might be called a national policy, moti-
vated by a mixture of reasons,- racial, economic, and
military. One of the weapons now used to secure this
elimination of the foreigner is the Important Industries
Control Law, which was promulgated in April, 193l. By a
decree of the Department of Commerce and Industry of
November,l932,this law was made applicable to persons
engaged in the gasoline trade who produce or sell as
much as 100,000 cases(ten gallon cases) per month.
Under this law persons in any of the industries de-
signated may enter into an agreement to stabilize con-
ditions in their fnaustry. A copy of any such control
agreerent must be sent to the Minister of Commerce and
Industry, though his permission need not be obtained
prior to the conclusion of the agreement. In the event
that he considers that the agreement is prejudicial to
the interests of the consumers or to the interests of
another closely connected industry, the Minister, on
the advice of a Control Committee in his department,
will order a revision or cancellation of the agreement.
In case one person, or firm, engaged in any of the
industries which are designated should refuse to enter
into the agreement or should break the agreement and
start what the others consider unjust competition, then
upon the application of two-thirds of the total number
of persons, or fimms, engaged in the industry, the Minister

=== Page 11 === (confidence 0.98)
- 9 -
may intervene and force the recalcitrant firm into
11ne.
The most important provision of the control
agreement entered into in December, 1932, by the six
firms engaged in the gasoline trade is the one which
fixes the price of standard gasoline at 48 sen per gallon.
This was a raise of 10 sen per gallon over the previous
price. However, the two foreign companies which import
gasoline state that they continue to sell at a loss when
the price is at this level and the Minister of Commerce
and Industry has been appealed to to allow an increase
of 8 sen per gallon. Great difficulty is being encountered
in getting approval for this raise and it is believed that
a raise of 3 sen is the most that will be obtained.
One reason given for the Minister's reluctance to
approve a further raise is the fear of disturbances
among the taxi-drivers and motorbus companies. There is
no denying that the situation in Japan in this respect
is very serious and this consideration is almost sufficient
to justify the Department's attitude. However, a further
statement was made by Mr. Fukuda, the Chief of the Mining
Bureau; viz, that the price would be kept as low as the
companies could bear. As the foreign companies are hardly
able to continue operations at the present price levels,
much less make a profit, the representatives of the two
foreign companies, who were interviewing him, then asked
what basis was taken in deciding the price levels the
companies could bear. He replied that the basis was the
cost

=== Page 12 === (confidence 0.99)
-10-
cost of production of gasoline in Japan, because (a)
the costs of production of the Japanese companies were
easier to investigate and (b) their trade was confined
to Japan and therefore the Japanese market was their
lifeline, while foreign companies had other markets in
which losses, if incurred in Japan, could be offset.
He furthermore pointed out that " it is natural for
any government to consider its own people first, this
nationalistic policy being fairly prevalent in the world
at present". From Mr. Fukuda the two foreign represen-
tatives went to Mr. Iwakiri, the Parliamentary Vice
Ministef of Commerce and Industry, who made a more
politic statement, saying that foreign company s
costs would be considered in deciding price levels.
Although Mr. Iwakiri is the higher ranking official
the two foreigners had the impression that Mr. Fukuda
was expressing the more accurate opinion. It is very
possible that the decision of the Government's "com-
mittee" regarding the future oil policy of the nation
is being awaited before a decision is made regarding
the price of gasoline. However, it seems hardly probable
that, in view of the disturbed social conditions in Japan,
the Government would allow the price of gasoline to be
raised to levels which would enable the foreign companies
to make a profit when a lower level would be enough for
the Japanese companies. It is more likely that the
question before the government's committee has to do
w1th

=== Page 13 === (confidence 0.97)
- 11 -
with encouraging or discouraging the establishment
of refineries by foreign companies;- unless, for some
spocial reason it is desired to continue the status quo
for a few years longer . The fate of the investments
in Japan of the two foreign companies may rest upon the
committee's decision.
Further evidence of the Japanese desire to become
independent of the foreign oil companies lies in the
attempts recently made to shift in part, at least, the
source of supply of crude oil from the United States
to Russia. These moves are interpreted to mean, first,
that certain Japanese refining and distributing companies
wish to obtain a supply of crude oil at a price which
will enable them to undersell in the local market the
American and Dutch gasoline long enough to force the two
foreign companies out of business. Secondly, they wish
to ease their dependence upon America for their supply
of crude oil.
Almost the entire 600,000 tons of imports of crude
oil in 1932 came from the United States, the principal
exception being some importations by the Navy from the
Dutch East Indies. A very high percentage of the fuel
oil also came from America, the remainder coming from
the Dutch East Indies. Of the gasoline, the United
States and the Dutch East Indies shared honors about
qually, the Socony-Vacuum getting its supplies from
the former and the Rising Sun obtaining its from the
latter souree. It is clear that the Japanese refineries,
a

=== Page 14 === (confidence 0.99)
- 12 -
as well as the Japanese Navy, have been dependent
largely upon the United States for their supply of
crude oil. Some of this oil has been purchased
by Japanese agents in California and some is sold through
American representatives in Japan, such as the Union
Oil Company which has a large office and staff in Osaka.
Recently two attempts have been made by the Japanese
to start a flow of crude oil and refined petroleum
products to Japan from the Baku district of Russia.
The low prices at which it is alleged that this oil
can be obtained are causing some concern to the American
sellers. However, the product must be transported
a long distance and it is not believed that the Russians
want to enter this market when there are so many others
nearer to the source of supply. There is a theory that
the Russians are preparing a market for the products of
their newly-built refineries in Vladivostok and Harbar-
ovsk which they hope to feed with crude oil from their
North Saghalien fields. However, this theory, if true,
would only explain the importations of refined products
from the Baku district, whereas the Japanese are interested
more in the supply of crude oil than in that of gasoline.
Last September much publicity was given to the
activities of Mr. Kojiro Matsukata, who signed a contract
with the Soviets for importing annually from the Baku
district 100,000 tons of petroleum products, mostly
gasoline. He is to deliver commodities, including
fishing

=== Page 15 === (confidence 0.98)
- 13 -
fishing instruments, in payment. Mr. Matsukata appears
to be confident that his project will meet with success.
However, the general opinion is that he has no capital
and the Japanese business men, as well as the Government
officials, seem to have no confidence in him. He has
no method of distributing his product in Japan and ap-
parently he has not yet been successful in selling his
product to any of the established distributing companies.
There are many rumors regarding his schemes to carry out
his contract without capital but the oil industry in
Japan now seems to have little fear of any competition
which he will give them. The general belief among the
petroleum companies is that he will be unable to get rid
of the first shipment and that the contract will fall
through. Their only interest is in seeing that the
Minister of Commerce and Industry forces him to sell
his gasoline at the price fixed in the control agreement.
More recently another contract has been signed with
the Russians and this one is causing much concern to the
trade. The North Saghalien Petroleum Company, which has
been mentioned earlier in this report, has signed a con-
tract with the Soviets for the purchase of 300,000 tons of
crude oil, fuel oil, and gasoline annually. A trial
shipment of 8,000 gallons of gasoline is now on its way
from the Black Sea. The officials of the Japanese Com-
pany contend that their object in signing the contract
was to combat Mr. Matsukata. It is believed that the
Nerth Saghalien Petroleum Company is not much interested
in selling Rassian gasoline in Japan as it will have to
1011

=== Page 16 === (confidence 0.99)
-14 -
sell the gasoline through the Nippon Oil Company
and other Japanese distributors which have their
own refineries, and who naturally prefer to sell
their owm gasoline products. What the Japanese
industry is interested in is crude oil for its
refineries. The shipment of the gasoline is pro-
bably to satisfy some demand of the Russians. As
the North Saghalien Petroleum Company is owmed by
the principal Japanese oil refining interests, its
intrest in purchasing cheap crude oil from Russia
is a threat both to the American companies which
have been selling crude oil to Japan and to the two
American and British companies who are trying to
compete against the Japanese refineries by importing
gaooline.
A further aspect from which to consider Japan's
oil problom is that of national defense. It is knowm
that the Japanese Navy has a large supply of oil
in storage at the present time. There are certain
difficulties in estimating the amount of these supplies
as the Navy's tanks are underground. However, after
studying reports from various sources the Naval Attaché
of this Embassy estimates that Japan probably has
2,000,000 tons of crude and fuel oil in storage. Ac-
cording to him this would keep the Japanese fleet going
on a war footing for from 30 to 35 months. However,
this does not take into consideration what might be
needed for commercial purposes during war time. In
ca80

=== Page 17 === (confidence 0.99)
-15-
case of a war North Saghalien could undoubtedly provide
more oil than it does now and the Japanese would probably
seize complete control of the product of the Russian
wells. Another source of supply of oil in the case of
a war would be the Dutch East Indies. The Navy's
tankers now bring from there crude oil to an unknowm
amount. Mr. Shiratori, the Foreign Office spokesman,
recently stated in a private conversation that the
Japanese Navy would seize the Dutch East Indies oil-
fields immediately on the outbreak of a war, no matter
who the enemy might be. Past experience has proved that
Mr. Shiratori's statements cannot be relied upon as
expressing any opinion other than his own. However,
it is interesting to know that this plan has even
been suggested.
Still another oil problem facing Japan at the moment
concerns Manchuria. A conference is now being held in
Tokyo in which this subject is being discussed and it will
be treated in a subsequent report. The plan now seems
to be to devise some scheme whereby the Japanese will
control the marketing of petroleum products in Manchuria,
to the exclusion of foreign companies, without violating
the Open Door policy.
In Japan Proper the American oil interests face a
definite move to oust them from their position. As far
as the gasoline trade is concerned a British firm will
share the same fate as the American one. Both of them
have considerable investments in distributing plants and
these investments are endangered. They are willing to
consider

=== Page 18 === (confidence 0.99)
-16-
consider investing more capital in refineries if the
Japanese state that they would welcome this move. This
would enable them to save what capital they have already
put into the country. It may be that they will find it
necessary to form companies with part Japanese capital
and sell out their interests to the new companies. In
fact, there is a strong feeling in some quarters that
this is the solution to their problem.
The American crude oil interests face serious
Russian competition. It remains to be seen whether
the Japanese importers of Soviet products can continue
to undersell the Americans. It will be especially
difficult if their source of supply is no nearer than
Baku. The possibilities of the Russian wells in North
Saghalien are unknown to outsiders. The Japanese have
not been working these wells to capacity but if the
Russians take them over they may not have the same
desire to conserve that supply. However, the Russians may
prefer to divert the crude oil from North Saghalien to
their owm refineries in Siberia rather than sell it to
the Japanese refineries. There is thus a possibility
that the Russians are planning to enter the Far Eastern
market seriously, in which case a new element is intro-
duced into an already complicated situation.
Respectfully yours,
Joseph C. Grew.

Despatch No. 366
Date: April 21, 1933
Subject: Japan's Oil Problems.
CLASSIFICATION CANCELLED Authority: letter
Strictly Comettal
of 5-20-64 from Donaid 1. Sincn, Siate Dept
B/DGruDate3)
Summary:
The Japanese are apparently contemplating the ousting
of the Socony-Vacuum Corporation and the Rising Sun Petro-
leum Company from the gasoline trade in Japan and are also
trying to shift, in part, the source of their supply of crude
oil from the United States to Russia.
The Navy receives some crude oil from North Saghalien
and Fushun, but also imports considerable quantities from
the Dutch Éast Indies and the United States. Gasoline and
other refined petroleum products are sold not only by the
two foreign companies mentioned above but also by certain
Japanese companies which do their refining in Japan. The
latter buy most of their crude oil from the United States.
Due to the gasoline tariff and the depreciation of the
yen the Japanese refineries can produce more cheaply than
the two foreign companies and the latter are contemplating
the building of refineries in Japan. However, they do not
wish to invest so much money in the country unless they
believe that the Government would welcome it and they are
trying to obtain a statement of the oil policy of the Govern-
ment. It is kmown that the Government is seriously studying
the oil question and it is probable that the two foreign
companies will be discouraged from taking any steps which
would give them a firmer foothold in the country. The
Government has already managed to place the foreign companies
in a disadvantageous position by means of the Important
Industries Control Law, which has only recently been made
applicable to the gasoline trade.
Two attempts have recently been made by Japanese oil
interests to import Russian crude oil into Japan. The first,
made with much fanfare by Mr. Matsukata, is not considered
threatening to the established trade as he has no capital
and does not enjoy the confidence of the business and official
interests. The second, made by the North Saghalien Oil
Company, may be successful if the Russians cooperate and
push the project.

Sir:
we are presentin for tur rev: ew  su mary of events
in Japan relatin in enera. t the oil incustry, and in particular
to the Socony-Vacuu.. Interests.
As a result of a chaotic conditien I ici extended over
many months and w ic.. was caused y disastrous competition follow-
ing depreciation in exchance and increased tariffs, the "Important
Industries Control Act" was invoked o  er ler 4th, 1932, to em-
5
brace the marketing of gasoline prcduced y the lar er Japanese
refiners and imported y the major fcreign companies. irior to this
a Gasoline Cartel was established by sue concerns, six in number.
of which the forei n interests were the isin sun letroleun Company,
Ltd., a subsidiary of the Asiatic Petroleum Company, Ltd., and
Socony-Vacuum Corporation. the Cartel in respect to selling prices
was placed un er the jurisdiction of the Department of Conmerce,
and in spite of every endeavor since then, it as leen impossible
to obtain the sanction of the Imperial Japanese Government for in-
creases to enable the inustry to market gasoline at a profit. To
illustrate the extent to Which prices have been depressed on
gasoline and other petroleum products, mey we point out thet our
operations in the Empire for the year 1932 resulted in a loss of
approximately 2,000,000. Our estimated loss for the first four
months of this year is alproximately 600,000.
FILED
For some time we have been endeavoring to obtain from
the Imperial Japanese Government an expression of their intentids
in respect to the policy to be applied to foreign interests of the
industry within the Japanese Empire. In this, His Excellency, the
American Ambassador at Tokyo, has been most helpful, although even
through his most courteous assistance, we have been una ble to ob-
tain any assurance that the Control Law mentioned above, will not
be used to the prejudice of foreign interests.
We have now been advised of certain announced policies
emanating from Japan in respect to a national oil policy which has
no doubt been brought to your attention, but for immediate reference
we quo te a telegram from our General Manager at Yokohama, under
date of May 8th, as follows:-

=== Page 32 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
Seoretary of State.
May 10/33.
"Refer to our telegram of lay 2nd local press has published
tentative plan national oil policy emanating from Department of
Industry outlining following alternative schemes both of which
we are informed would first require approval of all departments
of government and then Diet sanction FIRST Government Monopoly
involving purchase of all foreign companies' interests
SECOND Licensing system based on and favoring existing refineries
and embodying policy of makin Japan more independent of petroleum
from American British interests SToP Informal inquiry by American
Embassy failed to secure denial that plan along sucn lines was
being considered but assurances were given by Foreign Office that
no aotion contemplated in immediate future and in whose opinion
Monopoly doubtful SToP Embassy has telegraphed Washington fully
today STop We believe the following may have influenced publica-
tion at this juncture FIRST Opportune in view of coming conference
Washington SECoND Impending local discussion regarding renewal
Gasoline Agreement THIRD Political action connected with resolution
FoURTH Recent inquiry by American Eritish Dutch Ambassadors."
While this is hardly the first announcement of a plan for the
consideration by the Imperial Japanese Government of a monopoly or a
licensing system, nevertheless and in spite of the assurances iven by
the Foreign Office at Tokyo in the cable quoted above, it is our belief
that in view of the general political and economic situation, the open
avowal and consummation of governmental control alon these lines is
far more imminent than heretofore.
We understand the Embassy at Tokyo has telegraphed you the
substance of the proposed oil policy. The Socony-Vacuum Corporation
Interests have long been established in Japan and our investment toward
the promotion of such Interests is now in excess of twelve millions of
dollars. Consequently, we cannot avoid viewing with grave concern these
developments which seemingly point to placing our interests in serious
jeopardy.
In these circumstances, we intended submitting for your informa-
tion and consideration a summary of the situation, and specifically,
the effect in relation to us of the enforcement of the "Important Indus-
tries Control Act". While there is no definite assurance that either
a monopoly or licensing plan will be enacted and promulgated, even the
present status is of serious moment and we are particularly anxious to
ascertain the intentions of the Imperial Japanese Government.
The Embassy at Tokyo is fully cognizant of the situation and we
have instruoted our General Manager to request His Exoellenoy, the
Ambassador, to cable Washington a summary of his views based upon de-
velopments up to the time of the monopoly issue. Therefore it is possible
that the correlating of this information will afford you an opportunity
of reviowing the prinoiples involved and of determining whether you con-
sider it advisable and desirable to incorporate the subject in the
Agenda of the Eoonomic Conference at Washington with the Japanese
Delogation.

oretary of State.
May 10/33.
In the event that you desire to include this matter as part of
your conversations with the Japanese Delegates, you may wish to afford
us an opportunity of presenting the subject before you in more detail,
in which case, we shall be pleased to have one of our representatives
prooeed to Washington for this purpose.
Sinoe writing the above, Japan cabled today to the effect that
a licensing system could probably be introduced at once provided it did
not involve tariff changes whic would require the sanction of the
Imperial Diet. Thus the question becomes of even greater importance and
we shall look forward with much pleasure to an expression of your
thoughts in relation thereto.

EMBASSY OF THE
UNITED STATES OF AMERICA
Tokyo, May 11, 1933.
No. 391.
Subject:
Japan's Oil Problems.
(Supplementary information)
F/HS
M RECD
A 6 35
Offiege of Ecenemic Adviser
JUN 23 1933
EPARTMENT OF STATE
894.6363/36
894.6363
Division of
FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
MAY 27 1938
artment of State
Cohry sent to foudommith
foru
The Honorable
JUL 17 1933
FILED
The Secretary of State,
Washington.
Sir:
I wish respectfully to refer to my despatch No. 366
'32
of Apr1l 21, 1933/entitled JAPAN'S OIL PROBLEMS. Due to
developments which have taken place since its preparation,
which are described in my despatch No. 384 of May 11, 1933,
it appears likely that the oil problem may arise at the
London Conference. Having in mind the possibility that
the information contained in the despatch may be used by
American representatives, the following supplementary
details are submitted for the Department's information.
1.

=== Page 55 === (confidence 0.98)
- 2 -
1. The original control agreement between
the six firms engaged in the gasoline trade- Socony-
Vacuum, Rising Sun, Ogura, Mitsubishi, Nippon and
Mitsui- was signed on October 21, 1932 but had actually
been in force since July 1, 1932. On August 24 the
authorities had sanctioned an increase of 7 sen per
American gallon in the price of gasoline. The Im-
portant Industries Control Law having been extended to
apply to the gasoline trade in November 1932, as
mentioned on page 8 of despatch No. 366, the authorities
permitted the companies to modify their agreement on
December 12 so as to allow another raise of 10 sen
per gallon. It will therefore be seen that the au-
thorities are not in principle opposed to increases
in the price of gasoline. Agitations among the taxi-
drivers and motorbus companies occurred on both occasions
that the price was increased, but the authorities stood
their ground and refused then to be intimidated.
2. Since preparing despatch No. 366 information
has been received which makes it appear uncertain whether
the contract between North Saghalien Petroleum Company
and the Soviet Government has yet been actually signed.
A trial shipment of 8,0o0 tons of gasoline is known to
be on its way from the Black Sea and it is considered
probable by persons engaged in the trade that a contract
will be signed by the Japanese representative who is
said to be in Moscow now. However, it is understood
that the original figure of z00,000 tons of crude oil,
fuel

=== Page 56 === (confidence 0.99)
- 3 -
fuel oil, and gasoline may be reduced before agreement
is reached.
It has also been learned that the Nippon Oil
Company, which owns the largest interest in North
Saghalien, is interested in importing some gasoline from
Russia as a temporary expedient. According to this
report, Nippon's own refineries haven't sufficient
capacity to provide the amount of gasoline it can sell
through its present distributing system. As the Soviet
product can be purchased cheaply Nippon is willing
for the present to purchase it, rather than enlarge its
plant. This is probably one reason why gasoline has
been mentioned in the projected contract with the
Soviets though the North Saghalien group are, as
stated in my previous despatch, interested primarily
in diversifying their sources of crude oil.
While on the subject of Soviet oil, it was not
strictly correct to mention in my previous despatch the
"newly built" refineries in Siberia. Accurate information
on this subject being meagre, it is not known how nearly
completed these projects are at the present time.
3. The import duty paid on petroleum products
varies according to the percentage of volume of dis-
tillates up to 2750 C. by fractional distillation. The
lowest form of crude oil(that containing less than 20%
of distillates) which is the principal import of the
Japanese refineries, pays a duty of Yen 2.295 per 100
gallons. The duty on crude oil and fuel oil increases
according

=== Page 57 === (confidence 0.99)
-4-
according to the percentage of distillates. Gasoline
pays a duty of Yen 10.80 per 100 gallons. At the time
these rates were fixed they were supposed to represent
the relative gasoline producing qualities of the various
grades of oil. However, modern improvements in refining
methods have enabled more gasoline to be extracted from
the oil and,therefore, the duties work to the disadvantage
of the importers of refined gasoline.
4. It was not strictly accurate to say on page
11 of my previous despatch that the United States and
the Dutch East Indies share honors about equally in the
Japanese importations of gasoline. The imports from the
Dutch East Indies have in recent years been greater
than those from the United States in the proportion of
about 6 to 4.
Respectfully yours,
Joseph C. Grew.

SUBJECT:
Japan's Oil Problems: Current Developments.
STRICTLY CONFIDENTIAL.
Offee of Foenamir: Adviser
F/HS
JUN 23 1933
PEPARTMENT OF STATE
MAY 2 6 55
M RECO

June
Division of
FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
NMAY 27 1933
894.6363
Department of State
Copyrust to toudomiita
The Honorable
The Secretary of State,
Washington.
Sir:
32.
With reference to my despatch No. 366 of april 21, 1933,
133
and my telegram No. 94 of May 8, 11 a.m., concerning the
1/
situation of the foreign oil companies in Japan, I endlose
herewith for the Department's information a copy of a i-
3
vate and confidential letter received from the British
Ambassador with regard to the representations which he
made to the Minister for Foreign Affairs in this connection.
The Dutoh Minister made similar representations on two
occasions

=== Page 59 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
occasions, on one of which he saw both the Minister and
Vice Minister for Foreign Affairs and pointed out that
the Japanese oil plans as published (referred to later in
this despatch) would create an unfortunate impression
abroad, particularly at the present time when Japan is be-
lieved to be working for international freedom of trade.
As stated in my telegram of May 8, 1l a.m., I also
talked informally with the Vice Minister for Foreign Af-
fairs regarding the future of the foreign oil companies
in Japan. I pointed out that the American oil companies
(principally the Socony-Vacuum Corporation) had invested
large sums of money here in equipment, and might make
further investments by increasing their equipment, which
would undoubtedly be in the interest of the Japanese them-
selves, but that the American companies could not take such
steps without first receiving some definite indication or
assurances as to the future policy of the Japanese Government
towards the foreign oil business in Japan. I mentioned the
fact that there were indications that the Japanese Government
had been investigating the whole question of an oil policy
and that a virtual monopoly might be created in favor of
Japanese firms in connection with the refining industry
which might effectively squeeze the foreign oil companies
out of the market. I said that the Socony-Vacuum Corpora-
tion had already addressed a list of questions to the
Minister of Commerce and Industry in order to obtain a basis
for formulating its own future policy, but the desired in-
formation had not been forthcoming. I pointed out to the
Vice Minister that it was not my wish to seem to try to in-
fluence the Japanese policy, but that I hoped that some
precise indications could be given to the American companies

=== Page 60 === (confidence 0.99)
- 3 -
as to whether the Japanese Government desired their coopera-
tion in the future or whether a situation would be created
which would render it impossible for them to compete with
the Japanese firms in the Japanese market. The American
companies, I said, were already operating at a loss owing
to the effect of the Important Industries Control Law
which had brought prices down to a level at which the foreign
companies could not sell with a profit. The Vice Minister
asked me to let him have copies of the letter from the Socony-
Vacuum Corporation to the Minister of Commerce and Industry
2/3/.
and of the reply thereto, which has since been done. Copies
of these letters are enclosed for the Department's informa-
tion.
On May 4, 1933, the Mining Bureau of the Department of
Commerce and Industry released for publication the drafts
of two plans for national control of the oil industry and
trade. Copies of an excerpt from the JAPAN ADVERTISER of
41.
May 5, 1933, containing a translation of the two plans,
are enclosed for the Department's information. The pre-
cise text of the plans has not been released and is not
available, but the texts as published in the vernacular
and English-language newspapers agree in general purport, al-
though varying in phraseology. As will be seen, the first
plan contemplates a governmental monopoly of the oil in-
dustry and trade, to be handled by a corporation of which
the Japanese Government will own a large share. Under this
plan the existing properties (oil storage plants, distri-
buting stations, service stations, etc.) of the foreign oil
companies are to be purchased by the Government. The second
plan

=== Page 61 === (confidence 0.99)
- 4 -
plan contemplates the control of the oil industry and
trade by means of a system of a system of licenses for the
importation of petroleum and petroleum products and for
the refining of crude oil and the erection of refineries.
This plan also calls for the development of sources of
supply of crude oil other than American and British, and
for the development of a process of liquification of coal.
Under this latter plan the importation of petroleum proucts
into Japan by the foreign oil companies could be gra ually
decreased as additional Japanese refineries become able
to supply the market, while the construction of refineries
in Japan by the foreign oil companies could be prevented
if deemed desirable. Both of the plans, therefore, con-
template the eventual elimination of the foreign oil com-
panies from the Japanese field, except for the sale to
Japanese refineries of supplies of crude oil.
Upon publication of these plans, the Embassy got in
touch with the Commercial Bureau of the Foreign Office, with
the American oil companies (Socony-Vacuum and Associated
Oil) and with the newspaper correspondents. From these
various sources the Embassy obtained the following informa-
tion.
While the Chief of the Mining Bureau of the Department
of Commerce and Industry denies that the plans as published
are official, they were given to the press by the Assistant
Chief of the Bureau and therefore may be considered as
emanating from that Bureau. The plans are only tentative
and have not received the consideration or approval of
other

=== Page 62 === (confidence 0.99)
- 5 -
other Governmental departments interested, but were pre-
pared in response to a resolution introduced into the
House of Peers last March and attached as a rider to the
annual budget bill. This resolution, stating that the
supply of petroleum being a matter of imperative necessity
from a military and industrial viewpoint, and the production
of oil in Japan being small and inadequate to meet the mili-
tary and industrial needs of the country, calls upon the
Government to take imnediate steps to exploit oil resources
not only of Japan but of all places within Japanese influence,
and at the same time to endeavor to carry out a scheme for
the liquification of coal. It appears that, in accordance
with this resolution, the plans in question were prepared
by the Mining Bureau of the Department of Commerce and In-
dustry after consultation with the Army and Navy fuel ex-
perts and a Fuel Investigation Commi:sion. The Mining
Bureau, in issuing the plans, admitted that they were tenta-
tive and vague, and that it would be months, or even years,
before they could be approved anc put into effect. The
Commercial Bureau of the Foreign Office stated that the
plans had not yet been referred to the Foreign Office and
that nothing definite ha therefore been done, but that in
any case the Foreign Office was opposed to the idea of an
oil monopoly. The Commercial Bureau, however, did not
deny that plans for governmental control of the oil industry
and trade existed. The Dutch Minister, who interviewed the
Minister and Vice Minister for Foreign Affairs in regard
to

=== Page 63 === (confidence 0.99)
- 6 -
to the plans, received assurances that the plans, if they
existed, were not yet under consideration by the Foreign
Office, but he also received the impression that the plans
had some basis in fact.
It appears that a monopoly plan, or a licensing plan
involving a change in the tariff, will require the sanc-
tion of the Diet, which dors not meet again until next
December, unless a special session is c lled. A licensing
plan not involving a change in the tariff, however, could
probably be put into effect t any time by order of the
Cabinet and by Imperial orcinance.
Projects for a governmert monopoly of the oil industry
and trade have been put forward from time to time for some
years past, but no definite action nas been taken on these
projects to date. For various reasons, however, the foreign
oil companies believe that the present plans, or at least
the licensing plan, will receive serious consideration and
may be carried out. The plans were compiled in response
to a definite resolution of the House of Peers and are not
merely the dreams of some underlings in Government service.
The people of the country are wrought up over the matter
of national defense, and it would be a simple matter to put
any oil policy into effect if based on the plea of nation-
al defense. Pressure has been brought to bear by the foreign
oil companies and their diplomatic representatives upon
Japanese governmental organs in an effort to obtain some
indication of the future Japanese oil policy, and this pres-
sure is apt to bring the whole question to a head. Moreover,
the

=== Page 64 === (confidence 0.99)
-7 -
the largest Japanese oil company is reported to be in an
unfavorable financial condition, and it is possible that
it is bringing pressure to bear upon the Government to
adopt some oil policy which will be more favorable to the
Japanese companies. As a result of these factors, the
foreign oil comoanies are not inclined to view the present
plans as lightly as they have similar monopoly plans in the
past, especially in view of the fact that the foreign oil
companies are faced with the necessity, beceuse of exchange
and tariff difficulties, of erecting refineries in Japan
in the near future, if they wish to retain a share of the
Japanese gasoline trade. They naturally co not wish to
make these investments if they are to be subjected in the
future to expropriation under a monopoly or to control
under a licensing system.
The Dutch Minister is inclined to believe that Japanese
governmental control of the oil trade by means of a monopoly
or a licensing system may be in contravention of a clause of
the Dutch-Japanese treaty of commerce. It is possible that
he refers to a clause similar to the first paragraph of
Article I of the American-Japanese Treaty of Commerce and
Navigation of l9ll. While this clause would not appear to
apply to a government monopoly, it might be considered to
apply to any licensing system which did not contemplate
equal advantages to American companies with those given to
Japanese companies.
Various reasons have been advanced by the oil companies
and others for the publication of the oil plans at this
particular time, when the Diet is not in session and no
definite

=== Page 65 === (confidence 0.98)
-8 -
definite action is likely. The theory is advanced that
the plans are intended to act as a trading point in the
coming Economic Conference at London, or as a ballon
d'essai to test the reaction abroa to Japanese rostriction
of the oil trade. It is possible th:t they constitute an
attempt to deter the foreign oil companies from further
expansion of their trade in Japan, pending the formulation
and sanction of a definite oil program by the Government.
It is also possible that the plans are a tentative answer
of the Mining Bureau to the queries put to the Bureau by
the oil companies for an indication of the Government's
future oil policy.
The foreign oil companies in Japan are inclined to
believe that the question of the future of their trade in
Japan will be coming to a hea in the not far distant future
and naturally they are much perturbed over the situation.
As they have been unable to obtain any satisfactory as-
surances from the Government regarding the matter, the
opinion is gaining ground among them that it should be dis-
cussed at the London Economic Conference and some attempt
made there to arrive at a definite basis for future trade
in oil and oil products. Here the oil companies have no
grounds for negotiation, as they have no authority to offer
concessions to the Japanese in return for a liberal oil
policy, but they believe that something may be done along
this line at the Conference. As stated in my telegram No. 97,
34
May 11, 1l a.m. I am informed that the British Embassy in
Tokyo

=== Page 66 === (confidence 0.97)
- 9 -
Tokyo has suggested this procedure to the British Foreign
Office, while the Rising Sun Petroleum Company has taken
up this possibility with the head office of the Shell in-
terests in London. I have not yet r ceived a recuest
from the American oil inte ests to suggest a similar plan
to the Department, but I am informed that the local office
of theSocony-Vacuum Corporation is in communic tion with
its heac office on the subject. I m keeping in close
touch with the local managers of Americ'n oil interests in
connection with the matter.
Respectfully yours,
ot
Joseph C. Grew

Private and Confidential.
Dear Grew,
I spoke to Count Uchida this morning at my
farewell visit about the oil and I should like to
let you know before I go exactly what I said.
I made no complaint as regards the treatment
which our oil interests had suffered but I pointed
out that they had invested a great deal of money in
this country and that they were quite ready to invest
more if they knew that the policy of the Japanese
Government would not be directed to squeezing them
out. In short, they were anxious to co-operate with
the Japanese in the oil industry and, if they made
fresh investments, it would really amount to the
Japanese getting a loan without having to go on to
the open market. This would be of considerable ad-
vantage to the country in its present financial position.
I knew that the Japanese Government were considering
their whole oil policy and I sincerely hoped that they
would be able to satisfy our oil people that that policy
was one of co-operation and one under which it would
be advisable for our oil interests to increase their
stake in Japan still further.
Count Uchida listened carefully to what I said
and confirmed the fact that the Japanese authorities
were studying the whole question of oil which was of the
first importance to the country. He would certainly
convey to the committee the message which I had given him.
I have given a copy of this letter to Snow for
the information of Malcolm and am writing a similar letter to
General Pabst.
As ever,
(Signed) F. O. Lindley
His Excellency
Mr. Joseph C. Grew,
United States Ámbassador,
TOKYO.

YOKOHAMA, November 29th, 1932.
His Excellency, Baron K. N kejima
Minister of Commcrce and Industry,
TOKYO.
Your Excellency:
By Notice No. 4: of the Lepartnent of Conmerce
and Industry dated 4th November the Lr for the Control
of Important Industries No. 40 d: ted 1931 w:s made annlicable
to the gasoline trade in so far as concerned me chonts or
companies who were producing or selling lo0,0 c: ses per
month.
Just prior to November 4th our comp nies with
four others had signed an agreement such as is defined in
Article 1 of the said Law and we have therefore been brought
within the provisions of the Law.
From our study of the Control Law itself and of
the explanation of the Law issued by the temporary Rationali-
zation Bureau our understanding of the position is that the
Law is intended to aid in the rationalization of industry,
on the one hand by encouraging the companies or persons en-
gaged in the various important industries to enter into gree-
ments amongst themselves with the object of organizing and
stabilizing their industries and on the other hand by pro-
tecting the consumer and the general public from nossible
abuse of such controlling agreements.
In order to f&cilitate the conclusion of such
agreements provision is made in Article 2 of the said Law
whereby a mjority opinion may in certain circumstances
and with the approval of the Minister of State be made binding
on the other persons engaged in that industry.
This Article introduces into any controlled
industry a very sweeping change in that no company can any
longer be sure of enjoying its former right of deciding for
1tself what is best in the general interests, in the interests
of the industry and of itself but may be required to fall
in with the majority opinion provided such opinion meets with
the approval of the Minister of State.
In publishing this explanation of the Control Law
the temporary Rationalization Bureau emphasized the great
change thus made and the sweeping character of the powers given
by the Law in this respect and we understand that so far as
possible it will be the wish of the authorities that companies
or persons engaged in an industry shall voluntarily come to
agreements.

=== Page 69 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
While recognizing the advantages of such a
arse and having full confidence that it is the wish of
ll the companies equally to act in the common interest and
in the best interests of the oil industry we can not dis-
guise from ourselves that honest differences of opinion may
arise which would require to be submitted to the competent
Minister of State.
In the circumstances and ir. view of the large
interests our companies have in the oil inoustry, the large
investments they have already made and will recuire to make
if they are to continue to play as large : part in the oil
industry of Japan as they do at present, we feel that it is a
matter of the greatest importance for us to know beforehand what
are the broad lines on which Your Excellency considers the
Japanese oil industry should be stabilized, rationalized and
developed. It is only if we know Your Excellency's views
and wishes in this matter that we can be sure of shaping our
course to comply with them, of protecting the interests of our
own companies and of justifying to our Principals the wisdom
of any policies or investments we may advocate to them.
It is with these objects in view that we venture
to address the following incuiries to Your Excellency in con-
nection with the Law in general and in particular with its
application to our own companies end we should greatly ap-
preciate the honor of your kinu assistance in msking the
points clear to us.
1.
The first and perhaps the most important question
that arises is whether it is the wish of the Imperial Japanese
Government that the refining of crude petroleum in Japan should
be developed as distinct from or at the expense of the importa-
tion of petroleum products already refined, and if so whether
any encouragement to be given to refining in Janan would apply
eyually to the refining of imported and domestic crude petro-
leum.
We would draw Your Excellency's attention to the
fact that the quantities of the various refined products that
can be obtained from a given quantity of crude petroleum are
limited and that it may be difficult at any particular time
to import the quantity of crude petroleum that will produce
refined products in the exact proportions required by the
Japanese market. There might therefore arise a shortage of
certain products or a surplus of others which would destroy
equilibrium and would not do away with the necessity of im-
porting refined products while leaving all the difficulties
of disposing in the domestic market of other products of local
refining which might be in excess of domestic needs.
The capital required for the erection of a
modern refinery is large and the progress and changes in re-
fining technique are rapid. On the other hand the building
up of an organization and facilities for importing and selling
rofindproducts is also costly and requires time, and there-
feme it is not easy to change from one policy to another
without careful consideration and due notice.
In

=== Page 70 === (confidence 0.99)
- 3 -
In the past there has been certain discussion
 the press as to the encouragement of home products and
definitions of home products have been attributed to persons
in important positions which might give the impression that
the official definition of home products depended not so much
on where the products were or!ginally produced as on the capital
holdings of the companies producing them.
We should be greatly obliged if Your Excellency
would inform us whether in the event of the Government wishing
to encourage the refining of imported crude petroleum in Japan
the products of such crude petroleum would be officially re-
garded as home products only if the majority of the shares of the
company refining such products was held by Japanese subjects
and the company itself were under Jpanese management. We have
heard the petroleum industry in J nan defined as the industry
of producing and refining in Janan, end we woulc r spectfully
ask Your Excellency whether such is the official definition:
that is to say whether the importation of refined products
as practised by our companies is regarded as part of the
Japanese petroleum industry and whether therefore our comoanies,
as importers, may or may not expect equal encouragement and
treatment as companies refining in this country.
III
Should it be the Imperial Government's wish to
encourage the refining of petroleum in Janan could Your Ex-
cellency advise us whether it is its intention to do so either
by changes in the Import Tariff or by & system of licenses.
Should the Imperial Government adopt the latter method, might
we expect notice to be given to the importing companies so as
to allow them time to erect refineries capable of turning out
the same quota of refined products as they were importing at
the time?
Our companies are anxious to do all they can to
co-operate with the authorities in the development of the in-
dustry. On the other hand they would ask Your Excellency to
recognize that they have already played a large part in the de-
velopment of the industry and have made large investments in
Japan, and they feel that so long as crude oil or its products
have to be imported into Japan they should receive equal con-
sideration and assistance with other companies engaged in the
industry. Moreover Your Excellency will realize that if the
importation of crude oil be indiscriminately encouraged it
might be that foreign sellers of crude oil who have made no invest-
ment in the country would be placed in a position of advantage
compared with our own companies.
We should greatly appreciate Your Excellency's

ask enlightenment on the following points in connection with

Industries:
(1)
Under the said Law we are required to report
changes in price to the Department concerned so that the

interests of the industry or the public, in which case sanction
would not be given.
(a)

(a) In the event of such a decision being made
could the Department agree to any company stopping sales if it

(b) Would a Company be free  il times to meet

Under Article 2 of the Law it is laid down that in

Should such majority of the companies in an industry

=== Page 74 === (confidence 0.99)
I presume you are already informed of the fact that
the Government is trying every possible means for the
protection and encouragement of the oil producing and
refining industries. As to what further steps we shall
take, we will decide it after thorough investigations
of various factors.
2.
Every country in the world is now carryin:  home pro-
duct encouragement campaign for different purposes, i.e.,
fostering of home industries, checking of inports, diminu-
tion of payments to be made to foreign countries, etc. The
significance of the said campaign differs, depenuing upon
its actual purpose. As for the determination of whether
a certain product is a home product or not, it is to be
made after studying the actual condition of the industry
concerned as well as the purpose above referred to.
3.
Persons engaged in any of the industries designated in the
Important Industry Contol Law are bound to send a notice
of the details of the control agreement they concluded to
the competent Minister, but they need not obtain his per-
mission prior to the conclusion of the said agreement
(refer to Art. 1 Law No. 40 promulgated on April 1st, 1931
as well as Arts. 1, ,s and 4 of each of the Ordinances of
the Department of Commerce and Industry, the Department
of Agriculture and Forestry and the Department of Communi-
cations).
In the event that the control agreement concerned is recog-
nized to be prejudicious to the legitimate interests of
general consumers of the industry concerned or another
industry closely connected with it, the competent Minister
will order revision or cancellation of the said agreement
after getting the matter considered by the Control Com-
mittee(refer to Art. 3 of Control Law), so in the case
of the gasoline industry, the application of the Control
Law to which recuires thorough previous consideration of the
relationship between the industry concerned and the con-
sumers, it is right that those desiring to conclude a
control agreement should previously inform the competent
authorities of the details of the proposed agreement,
and decide(to conclude it) after obtaining their under-
standing, since both the competent authorities and the
parties to the proposed agreement will be able to save un-
necessary trouble and the agreement be smoothly operated.
4.
In case a person engaged in any of the industries
designated by the Government should start an unjust com-
petition and thereby upset the control of the industry
concerned, and not less than two-thirds of the same
industry apply to the competent Minister for remedial
steps, and the Minister recognizes the necessity of
protecting the legitimate interests of the said industry
and thereby conducing to a healthy development of the
national economy, he(the Minister) may order the parties
to the agreement concerned as well as any person outside
it to observe the agreement after getting the matter con-
sidered by the Control Committee(refer to Art.2 of the
Control Law and Art. 5 of the Departmental Ordinance
concerned).

=== Page 75 === (confidence 0.97)
-2 -
5.
The reason why the application of the Control Law
has been confined to producers or dealers of a capacity
of not less than lo0,oo0 cases a month is because, judging
from the present conditions of the industries subject
to the Control Law, we have considered it possible to effect
an adequate control of the industry concerned even by
limiting the scope of the application of the said Law
to the extent above referred to.
However, in the event that any person not covered by the
preceding paragraphs acts in such a way  to menace the
existence of the established control greement, we may
so revise the eligibility to the nrotection of the Control

JAPAN MAKES PLANS TO MONOPOLIZE OIL.
JAPAN ADVERTISER, May 5, 1933.
Commerce and Industry Officials Want
Either State Monopoly or License System
May Oust Foreign Firms.
Authorities Regret Publication of News,
Which They Declare to Be Premature.
Tentative plans for State control of a li-
cense system, the eventual purpose of which would
be to eliminate all foreign companies from this mar-
ket, are being considered by the Japanese Government.
Japan hopes to become self-sufficient as to oil and a
tentative plan for reaching this objective has been
drawn up by the Ministry of Commerce and Industry.
The Mining Bureau of the Ministry acting under in-
structions of the fuel section of the Commerce and Industry
Investigation Commission, has drafted two plans, one for
State Control and the other for a license system, accord-
ing to Rengo. These plans will be studied in detail by the
authorities of the Finance, War,Navy and Overseas Ministries.
Details of the State control plan follow:
1. All rights of crude oil production, oil exports
and imports and sales of oil are to be a monopoly of the
State.
2. A large oil company will be established under
joint Government and private management.
3. The company shall contribute to the Government a
sum equivalent to the difference between present cost of
doing business and the (expected) lower cost of doing busi-
ness under joint control. The company will pay no import
tariff and rationalization is expected to cut costs.
4. This money and the dividends on shares owned by
the Government shall be used by the Government to erect a
factory for low temperature carbonization of coal and to en-
courage the manufafture and use of fuel substitutes.
5. The Government shall force foreign oil companies
to sell it the shares they hold in Japanese oil companies.
6. The Government company shall spend a certain amount
of money each year on the dewelopment of Japan's oil re-
sources

=== Page 77 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
arces, before paying dividends.
7. The consolidated company shall be established
in the following manner:
(a) The Government shall force foreign companies
to sell it what assets they have in Japan and shall turn
these over to the new com any in exchange for shares. The
combined interests of the Japan Oil, Mitsubishi Oil, Ogura
Oil, Rising Sun Petroleum and Socony-Vacuum companies are
valued at Yen 160,000,000, with the 1ast two companies ac-
counting for Yen 40,000,000 of the total.
(b). Japan Oil, Mitsubishi Oil, Ogura Oil and all
other Japanese producing and importing cnncerns shall be
included in the amalgamation.
8. The Government shall retain the right to super-
vise cisposal of profit, new enterprises, appointment and
dismissal of directors and the obligation to bore new wells
in Japan.
This is the monopoly plan. Details of the license
system scheme follow:
1.
A license system shall be adopted for imports
of oil.
2. Those who pro uce oil at their own refineries
must have licenses.
3. Licenses shall be required for erection and ex-
pansion of oil refining equipment.
4. Imports of crude oil shall be free or subject
to very low tariffs, while imports of refined oil shall be
trstricted by very high tariffs.
5. In order to assure supplies of crude from sources
other than the United States and Great Britain, the follow-
ing methods shall be adopted:
(a). There shall be imoorts from the Soviet Union,
Rumania and other countries.
(b). The Government shall encourage the North Sagha-
lien Oil Company to drill new wells in its most promising
fields.
(c). The Borneo Oil Company shall be encouraged to
drill new wells.
(d). A syndicate shall be formed to invest in foreign
011

=== Page 78 === (confidence 0.99)
- 8 -
oil companies.
(e). Efforts shall be made to do further experi-
mental drilling in Hokkaico, Formosa and South Saghalien
with the idea of increasing domestic production in as
brief a period as possible.
6.
The Gove'nment shal! erect low temperature
coal carbonization and liquification plants with money
obtained by contributions from the chart red companies
and with certain funds set aside from the customs revenues
and shall force the chartered companies to assume the
obligation of storing the crude oil.
The facts above were released by Rengo and printed
in all Japanese papers yesterday. Authorities of the
Mining Bureau of the Ministry of Commerce and Industry
told a representative of The Japan Advertiser yesterday
that these plans were simply tentative and that they pro-
bably would not be put into effect for & year or so.
They declared that elaborate study as to the difficult
question of the foreign companies would be necessary.
They seemed rather perturbed that the newspapers had pub-
lished their plans, for they thought the matter had been
given publicity too soon.

Date: May 11, 1933.
Subject: Japan's Oil Problems: Current Developments.
Summary:
As explained in despatch No. 366, of Apri1 21,
1933, the foreign oil companies are worried
about their future in Japan, and have been en-
deavoring to obtain an indicntion of the future
Japanese oil policy. In pursuance of this en-
deavor, the British an? American Ambassadors
and the Dutch Minister at Tokyo have discussed
the matter informally with officials of the
Foreign Office. A few days later the Mining
Bureau of the Department of Commerce and Indus-
try allowed the publication of two tentative
plans for governmental control of the oil in-
dustry and tra e, one provi ing for a govern-
ment monopoly of oil in Japan and the other
proviing for control by means of a licensing
system which could be used to exclude foreign
importers of petroleum products and foreign
refiners from this market. It was found that
these plans were compiled i" response to a re-
solution of the House of Peers, and for various
reasons the foreign oil companies fear that there
may  e so e real intent to adopt an oil policy
whioh will eventually rive them from the field.
It is possible that the oil plans were released
for publication at this particular time to af-
ford a trajing point at the Economic Confe.ence,
or to deter the foreign companies from further
expansion until efinite schemes are formulated,
or as an answer to thebil companies' request for
an indication of Japan's future oil policy. The
oil companies believe that an attempt should be
made at the coming economic conferences to ob-
tain, by means of trading concessions, some as-
surance from the Japanese of an oil policy which
will permit the continuance of their trade in
Japan.

to exclude foreign oil companies from operation
in Manchuria. Several meetings have been held
and a new company projected in which the South

The purpose of the comoany is to exploit the oil

censèng system established in Manchuria to con-
control the distribution of oil products. They
hope eventually to be able to "squeeze out" the

forward by the Rising Sun Petroleum Company(British) and

the Socony-Vacuum Corporation(American) that the whole
matter of the future of foreign oil companies in Japan
be discussed at the London Economic Conference.

The two foreign oil companies have now further
developed their original idea into a proposal that the
Japanese Government be requested to send representatives
of the leading J panese oil componies to London, if possible
during the Economic Conference, for the purpose of dis-
cussing with American, British und Dutch representatives
of oil interests some plan which will be fair to all oil
interests in J-pan end which will sofeguard the investments
and trade of the foreign comp-nies in Japan. This proposal

has been unable to confirm.

(similer letter sent to General Pabst)
Private & Confidential
13th Apr11,1933
Dear Grew:-
I spoke to Count Uchida this morning at my fare-
woll visit about the oil and I should like to let you know
before I go exactly what I said.
I made no complaint as regards the treatment which
our oil interests had suffered but I pointed out that they had
invested a great deal of money in this country and that they were
quite ready to invest more if they knew that the policy of the
Japanese Government would not be directed to squeezing them
out. In short, they were anxious to co-operate with the Japan-
ose in the oil industry and, if they made fresh investments, it
would really amount to the Japanese getting a loan without
having to go on to the open market. This would be of considerable
advantage to the country in its present financial position. I
knew that the Japanese Government were considering their whole
oil poliey and I sincerely hoped that they would be able to
satisfy our oil people that that policy was one of co-operation
and one under which it would be advisable for our oil interests
to increase their stake in Japan still further.
Count Uohida listened carefully to what I said and
oomrimed. the faot thet the Japanese authorities were studying
mele question of oil which was of the first importance to
eountry. He would certainly convey to the committee the

=== Page 95 === (confidence 0.97)
moesage which I had given him.
I have given a copy of this letter to Snow for
the information of Malcolm, and am writing a similar letter to
General Pabst.
(Signed) F.O. LINDLEY.

Mr. Sahsbury:
I have examined the attached file concerning the
894.6363/45
894.6363
diffioulties of the Standard Oil Vacuum Corporation in
Japan. In view of the fact that the parties concerned
in this diffioulty are apparently now engaged in nego-
tiation for a settlement, there appears to be nothing
whioh the Department can properly do at the present time.
It ocours to me, however, that should the Japanese Gov-
ernment carry out the plan suggested for the national-
ization of the petroleum business, the Standard Oil Vacuum
Corporation may return to the Department with a request
for assistance. In case such a request should be received,
I think it would be desirable for the Department to ask
the Corporation to furnish as complete information as pos-
sible concerning the terms of any arrangement whioh the
Corporation made with the Japanese Government before es-
tablishing itself in Japan. The Corporation must have
obtained either a concession contract under which it oper-
ates or it must have obtained permission to construot
1
2
storage facilities and other necessary plants for carrying
19
on its business. If the Japanese Government should, in
connection

=== Page 124 === (confidence 0.94)
-2
comnection with any proposed plans for nationalization
of the industry, violate the terms under whioh the Ameri-
can corporation established itself in Japan, the Corpor-
ation would apparently have a reasonable basis of complaint
and probably a basis for a claim.
I do not think that the Department should take any
steps to obtain this information at the present time but
it may be well to keep in mind the necessity of obtaining
it should further difficulties develop.

Dear Dr. Hornbeok:
Following our letter of July l9th, our Yokohama Office
have cabled us under date of July 25th, as follows:-
"Information as per our telegram of July 13th was cabled to
London who replied substantially as follows requesting friends
to consult with us QUOTE As we believe British Dutch American
Governments do not wish at present to add to difficulty with
Japan we propose approach Manchurian Government direct to
ascertain FIRST Their definite plans SECONDLY If they are
prepared to allocate any part of trade to us SToP We propose
to apply for permission to erect small refinery at Hulutao or
Newchwang where we have land SToP We feel application will do
no harm and if permission is granted believe Government will
have difficulty in overlooking such domestic refinery in favor
of one at Dairen and that our action may force declaration of
future policy SToP Is it possible to do anything with Manchurian
FILED
Minister Tokyo END OF QUOTATION Friends have replied to the
effect that ▲ They consider declaration by Manchurian Government
regarding allocation of trade unlikely until details of Monopoly
plan settled but that B Applications for refinery might force
some definite indication of future policy C Consider it inadvis-
able question Manchurian Minister until after London has filed
refinery application."
The message to London, mentioned in the first line above,
was sent to the head office of the Asiatic Petroleum Company and it
would appear that the British oil interests either approached their
government or the British Delegation to the London Conference. From
the text it is possible that the Dutoh and American representatives
expressed an informal opinion to the effect as stated. Beyond that
we believe that the idea of approaching the "Manchoukuo" government
must have been conceived only by the British interests, for we cannot
imagine any of the representatives of the governments concerned could
have made this suggestion.
You will gather of course, that it is the Asiatic
Petroleum Company who propose applying for permission to erect a
small refinery at Hulutao or Newohwang. This in itself, and as an in-
dependent action, is quite a logical move, but we are very much

=== Page 126 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
F. Hornbeok.
July 28/33.
inolined to agree with the Asiatio representative in Japan that any
deolaration by "Manchoukuo" in respect to the participation of trade
would be most diffioult to secure. Further, it seems to us that an
approach to the "Manohoukuo" government, either before or after fil-
ing a refinery application, would hardly be consistent or compatible
with the "Open Door" policy, inasmuch as we believe none of the for-
eign governments which might be involved, and particularly that of
the United States and Great Britain, have recognized "Manohoukuo" as
an entity.
We have the thought that procedure along the lines sug-
gested should heve very careful consideration so that any ultimate
steps by the Government of the United States, and incidental ly, that
of Great Britain, would not be jeopardized or in any way nullified
by independent action of strictly commercial companies.
In these circumstanoes, and if you conour with our
thoughts, an unofficial expression of opinion which can be paraphrased
in a cable to our Japan representative, will be very deeply appre-
ciated. Of course, we would take the utmost precaution to have it
appear that any suggestions expressed by us emanated from this office
and not from Washington, inasmuch of course, as the contemplated plan
is one of British conception and does not directly affect us at the
moment, although eventually, this may be the case. We ven ture to say
that you will understand our position when we make the observation
that we might be able to bring some influence to bear upon the British
interests, particularly if there is any doubt in their minds that the
proposed action may possibly exert a deterrent reaction upon the
final solution of the entire Manohurian situation.
In my last letter, I spoke of Mr. Horinouchi, the Japan-
ese Consul General at New York. It might be of more than passing in-
terest to no te the remarks of our Moukden Manager, Mr. Loucks, in
regard to Mr. Horinouohi, when some question arose respecting the at-
titude taken by "Manohoukuo" officials concerning customs duties. Mr.
Louoks was informed by Mr. Miura, formerly Japanese Consul at Moukden
and now Seoretary of the Embassy at Changohun, that Mr. Horinouchi
very closely watohed the trend of American press dispatohes, and as
he was extremely friendly toward any Amerioan efforts to trade with
"Manohoukuo", he oocasionally submitted suggestions to his Rmbassy
at Changohun for transmittal to the "Manchoukuo" government.
We have no intention of fully discussing our diffioulties
with Mr. Horinouohi as we feel such matters more rightly belong to the
regular diplomatio channels. Nevertheless as this gentleman is in
our opinion, very favorably disposed to Amerioan interests, and as
his baokground is such that he is in a position to acquaint his
Government either direotly or through the Bmbassy, with pertinent
data affecting relations between Japan and the United States, we be-
lieve that the cultivation of his acquaintance might conceivably
prove of advantage to us.
