Dr. Hornbook.
Of partioularly grave concern to us is the allegation that
the companies now operating in Manchuria will be permitted to supply
only one-half of the market requirements of White produots(Gasoline
and Illuminating Oil), while the balance is to be drawn from the
proposed company. Our figures indicate that for the year l932 the
Japanese distributing companies in Manchuria shared 17.6% and 10.6%
in the total trade of Kerosene Oil and Gasoline respectively. The
peroentages for the current year to date, are approximately the same,
whereas Socony-Vacuum Corporation's participation in the indicated
total trade for the first five months of this year is:-
Kerosene O11
28.6%
Gasoline
27%
Thus the consummation of the Japanese plan would seriously interfere
with our business and also with that of the British.
We realize of course, that at present we are dealing only
with a possible eventuality, and that no action of any kind can be
taken until the project has the official sanotion of the "Manohoukuo
Government and is in actual operation. If such a contingency arises
we then assume that our Government will be in a position to put forth
representations predicated upon the policy of the "Open Door".
Relevant to the entire question of a petroleum policy for
Japan and Manchuria, perhaps you may be interested in a paraphrase
of Mr. Horinouchi's remarks, who, as you no doubt are aware, is the
Japanese Consul General in New York. A friend and I had luncheon
with him today and he seemed to be rather frank in discussing the
matter. In respect to Japan, he stated he did not feel that the
Government would declare a monopoly, but inasmuch as many other gov-
ernments had established a nationalistic oil policy, he could not be
certain as to what steps would be taken in regard to the institution
of a licensing system. He no doubt had in mind the systems in force
in Franoe and Spain. This simply strengthens our belief that ultim-
ately some disoriminatory barrier will be erected against foreign
interests.
You will perhaps remember, when I had the pleasure of talking
with you in Washington, I made the observation that if it were pos-
sible to intimate to the Japanese Government that silk might become
a vulnerable issue in the event of undue disorimination against this
country, that we would have a bargaining point of tremendous import-
ance. My companion at luncheon, who is not in the oil business,
happened to touch upon this subjeot, pointing out the value of raw
siik importations into the United States which enter duty free, and
briefly dwelling upon the fact that seri-oulture would be adversely
affected if an American tariff were imposed. It is conceivable that
Mr. Horinouohi will aoquaint the Japanese Ambassador with certain of
these remarks.

=== Page 129 === (confidence 0.98)
-3-
Dr. Hornbeck.
July 19/33.
Mr. Horinouchi's comments in respeot to Manohuria were what
might have been expeoted under the ciroumstances. He stated that the
Japanese Government has no intention of abrogating the "OpenDoor"
polioy and he felt that operations of foreign interests in that
country would not be restrioted in any way. Further, because of its
undeveloped state, foreign capi tal would be welcomed. Our own
thought is that the latter might possibly be true in some respects,
but that the former is decidedly questionable under present con-
ditions.
In contrast with Mr. Horinouohi's expressed opinion on the
"Open Door" in Manchuria, we recently talked with Mr. James L. Young,
who during the absence of Mr. B. W. Fleisher, proprietor and editor
of "The Japan Advertiser" in Tokyo, has been in charge of this Amer-
ioan publication, which no doubt is familiar to you. He has had
considerable contact with the Foreign Office and the impression he
gathered was that the Japanese were rather reticent in reaffirming
this polioy under the present Manchurian regime. In faot, we under-
stood his opinion to be that the Japanese would not hesitate to
discriminate against foreign interests. Of oourse, we should prefer
not to be quoted in regard to either of these convarsations.
We shall take the liberty of again communicating with you as
soon as we have further information of importance.
Sincerely yours,

During your temporary absence, the

letters from Socony under dates July 27
and 31 that they wish the Department
to give them an unofficial expression
of opinion which they can pass on to
their office in Japan.
It is my opinion that we should not
attempt to give any such opinion in
writing. Personally, however, I Foel
that the Asiatic Petroleum Company
would be unwise to attempt to foroe
issues at this time. To do so will not
deter monopoly action, if such action
has been deoided upon by the Japanese,
and, in addition, there appears to be
dissension among the participants of the
proposed monopoly. Furthermore, the
Japanese may decide against any such
seheme as the official announcement of
such a monopoly would materially weaken

SUBJECT:
The Oil Business in Manchuria.

Department of State
893.6363m
894.6363/49
The Honorable
The Secretary of State,
Washington.
Sir:
With reference to my despatch No. 399 of May 12,
1933, I wish to submit to the Department further in-
formation regarding the oil business in Manchuria,
which this Embassy has obtained from a source found
in the past to be reliable.
It is reported that it has now been definitely
decided to form a company for the purpose of building
OCT 1 4 1933
FILED
and operating an oil refinery at Dairen, not at Hulatao
as

=== Page 144 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
as reported in the press. In fact, a site opposite
the property of the Socony Vacuum Corporation at
Dairen has already been selected and the land pur-
chased with funds advanced by the South Manchuria
Railway. The company will be capitalized at Yen
5,000,000. The South Manchuria Railway will sub-
scribe to two million yen of this, "Manchukuo" to
one million, and the following Japanese oil com-
panies to five hundred thousand each: Mitsui
Bussan, Mitsubishi Mining Company, Ogura, and
Nippon. At first, the refinery will purchase
its crude oil from abroad from the source that
gives the most advantageous rate- pending the
development of sources within Manchuria. The
Nippon Oil Company will supply the skilled labor
and technicians for building and starting the
operation of the refinery. It is hoped to have
the refinery completed during the autumm of this
year.
The next problem before the originators of
this scheme is that of assuring the refinery of a
market in Manchuria without violating the principle
of the Open Door. It is understood that they have
tentatively decided upon a plan of governmental
control under which the refinery would be guaranteed
one half of the market and the commercial companies
now operating in Manchuria the other half. A "Man-
chukuo" controlled organization, called a "monopoly",
will be established to purchase the product of the
refinery

=== Page 145 === (confidence 0.96)
-3-
refinery and to market it in "Manchukuo" territory.
It is not known whether the commercial companies will
be compelled to sell to this so-called monopoly or
whether they will be licensed to sell fixed amounts
through their om established distributing systems.
The private companies will pay duty on what they
import whereas the "Manchukuo" monopoly will pay no
duty on what it imports from the refinery, even
though the latter bein, in Kwantung Leased Terri-
tory is outside the "Manchukuo" customs zone. It
would seem to be unimportant whether "Manchukuo"
gains its revenue in the form of customs duties or
in the form of excess profits, as long as the sell-
ing price of petroleum products is sufficiently high
to enable the commercial comnanies to make a profit.
It is understood that throughout the negotiations
for the formation of the refining company and the es-
tablishment of the "Manchukuo" so-called monopoly the
Japanese military and the South Manchuria Railway
have been fighting each other for control. The
military work through "Manchukuo".

Respectfully yours,
IoseLT. J

roeus
Tokyo's dospatoh No. 562 of Ootober 26,
1933, brings up to date recent developments
relating to the Japanese oil industry. It
is an interesting despatch on an important
-subjeot, and should be read in full.
It will be noted that the principal
result of the marketing of Soviet gasoline
in Japan has been to accelerate the tendency
towards some form of coordination between,and
oven possibly unification of,the companies
engaged in the produotion ana sale of
gasoline. The leading Japanese companies
have reduced their prices in order to meet
the prioes of Soviet gasoline sold through
Mr. Matsukata. These prices are apparently
below the levels at which gasoline can be
profitably sold, as it is pointed out by the
Imbassy that the Government must come to their
rescue before these Japanese companies
collapse.
An inter-departmental committee on oil
has not arrived at any decision with regard to
the national oil policy, but apparently the
idea of amalgamating ali the companies, foreign
as

=== Page 166 === (confidence 0.99)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
- 2 -
as well as Japanese, into one company, finds
considerable favor. One suggestion is that
the oxisting companies should transfer their
properties to the proposed company and receive
shares in the latter in payment thereof. As
the assets of the American and British com-
panies are valued in foreign currenoy, the
Rmbassy believes that great difficulty would
be experienced in arriving at some satisfactory
valuation in terms of yen of their properties.
The foreign companies may have no alternative,
as the principal Japanese companies are
reported to have agreed to merge and to have
oxpressed the hope that the foreign companies
and similar Japanese companies be permitted to
onter the merger. In either case it seems
very likely that the plan is to eliminate the
foreign companies from active participation
in the gasoline business. It is to be noted
that the Japanese Navy Department is opposed
to the plan for the control of the gasoline
trade, as the Japanese Navy fears that its
souroes of crude oil would be cut off in
Fetaliation for the injury done to the foreign
gasoline companies operating in Japan.
Your particular attention is drawn to the
last paragraph of the despatch, in which the
suggestion of the local manager of the Socony
Vaouum Corporation is put forward for
seouring assistance from the American Govern-
ment. The suggestion is that "the Japanese
might be required to import a certain amount
of gasoline if they wished to import crude
oil" from the United States.
The

-3-
The question of transmitting this
suggestion to the NRA or to any other appro-
priate body should be decided by conference
with RA.

Subject:
Japan's Oil Problems.
133
He
O-ronm Avisar
Division of
FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS

NOV 1S 1933
LHD
894.6363/54
Departrment of State

2
EPARTMENT OF STATE
The Honorable
MAR 27
The Secretary of tatc,
FILED
washington.
191
Sir:
I have the honor to r ler to y revious despatches
on the anove subject, the lest of which was No. S of
September 15, 1933, tno to renort to the Depurtment the
following developments which hove recently ti ken olace.
The two great problems now confronting Japan's oil
inaustry are the crisis produced by the introuuction of
Soviet gasoline into the market by r. Matsukata and the
efforts of the Government to bring the oil industry under
the

=== Page 169 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
the control of its own nationals as much as possible.
The latter problem is one of long-standing but it is
being brought to a head by the appearance of the former.
Mr. Matsukata continues to sell his gasoline at prices
well below the cost of production of his competitors, the
established companies. However, as has already been re-
ported, they have lowered their prices in oroer to compete
with him. As a consequence, though Mr. Matsukata sold
about 500,000 gallons in the month of Seotember, his sales
have been declining since that time. He does not possess
a distributing system capable of competing with those of
the Rising Sun, Socony, and the four larger Jananese
companies. Consequently it may be said that the effects
of his action in underselling his competitors have been
to injure them and  pparently do very little permanent
good to himself and his principals.
This Embassy is informed that when  r. Matsukata
was recently secretly approached by a group of labor leaders
who feared that their men woula lose their jobs if the
established companies were driven out or business and who
wished to mediate between the two groups, he replied that
they should not attack him but should drive"the foreigners"
out of the country. He stated that it was the est:blished
companies, led by the two foreign companies, who had lowered
prices first. He was unuoubtedly referring to the lowering
of prices which the Japanese companies forced upon the
foreign companies, against their wishes, iinnediately upon
the importation of Mr. Matsukata's proouct. This was
explained

=== Page 170 === (confidence 0.99)
- 3 -
explained in my last despatch on this subject. However,
the interesting point is that now Mr. Matsukata is apparently
trying to arouse the labor element against the foreigners
by placing upon the latter the blame for the price war.
At the time the last agreement under the Important
Industries Control Act was entered into by the established
companies, the Department of Commerce and Industry promised
that it would soon take some action in regard to Mr.Matsu-
kata. However, there has been a noticeable disinclination
on he part of the Government to interfere with him. Mr.
Matsukata is a man of some influence in governmental
circles but it is believed that there are three other
important reasons for the Government's nolicy. First,
it is afraid of the consumers. The motorbus and taxicab
owners are well organized and are in a position not only
to bring considerable pressure in orficial circles but also
to cause serious disturbances among the people. Secondly,
some elements in the Government are content to see con-
fusion in the gasoline trade in the hopes that it will
cause the several compnies to cooperate with the Govern-
ment in its schemes for control of the industry. Thirdly,
there are wide dirferences of opinion anong the Government
departments. The Department of Commerce and Industry, the
Foreign Office, the Finance Department, the War Office,
and the Navy Department do not see eye to eye in the matter
of an oil policy and the Government has decided that they
must all agree on a policy before one can be adopted.
Although all the established g soline companies are
hit by Mr. Matsukata's operations, the Japanese companies,
the

=== Page 171 === (confidence 1.00)
the most important of which are Nippon, Mitsubishi, and
Ogura,- have smaller resources to carry through a price
war than have the larger foreign companies,- the Rising
Sun and the Socony Vacuum. As the three Japanese com-
panies are the kernel of the Government's schemes and the
basis of its dreams of bringing the refining business under
Government control, it behooves the Government to come to
their rescue before they collapse. Consequently, the
Government departments concerned have been very active
trying to formulate an oil policy for the country.
The Department of Commerce and Inaustry, because
of its duty of supervising the operation of the agreement
between the companies under the Important Inuustries Control
Law, is more concerned with the immediate problems. How-
ever, there is an interdepartmental conmittee working
on the question and it is understood that it is drafting
a basic law for submission to the next session of the Diet.
The Minister of Commerce and Industry recently informed a
representative of the foreign gasoline inter sts that the
Government wants to amalgamate all the companies engaged
in the gasoline trade in Japan.
From other sources it has been learned that the
plan being considered is to form a large private company,
the stock of which could be held by foreigners as well as
by Japanese, but with Japanese control. The French Govern-
ment plan is being used as a model. The Foreign companies,
as well as the Japanese, would be asked to accept stock in
payment for their property in Japan. Dividends would be
divided

=== Page 172 === (confidence 0.98)
- 5 -
divided under the direction of the Government. Importing,
selling, and refining oil products would be subject to
licensing and only this new company would be licensed to
own refineries . The tariff woulo be rovi:eu to make a
greater difference between the auty on erude oi! tnd that
on the refined product tnan exists t present. No est b-
lished company would be forceu to enter the combine but it is
obvious that those on the outside would -ventuelly be "srue zed
out" of the market. If the two foreign como nies should
express an interest in entering the combine thore would rise
the tremendous questions of personnel and the valuation of
their property. The Socony Vacuum Corporation's property
is valued on the comp:ny's books in terms of dollars. The
property was bought with yen of ne rly twice the present
value of the yen in terms of doll:rs. The problem of valuating
the property in terms of the stock of the new company would
be very intricate. Yet, if the two foreign companies do not
enter the combine they are faced with the probability of
losing their business by a gradual process.
The above outline of the plan under consideration was
recently given out by an official of a Japanese Oil Company
as extremely confidential information. However, on October
24 a report appeared in the press that the three Japanese
oil companies had agreed to merge and had expressed to the
Minister of Commerce and Industry the hope that later the
American and British companies and 20 smaller Japanese
companies might be allowed to enter their combine. It would
therefore seem that the great combine is to be reached by
gradual

=== Page 173 === (confidence 0.98)
- 6 -
gradual stages.
It would seem that the o ject of the Japanese
Government is to bring the refining industry in Japan
under the control of Japanese nation: 1s with as little
cost as possible. As capital is obtained for builoing
refineries the Goverrment wants them to be built and
wishes to give them full protection. This is a plan
which has been followed by many countries of the world.
A feature of the Japanese problem which is not true of
all countries is that Janan's orincipal sources of supply
of the raw m terial are not under her control. In fect,
the three sources,- the United States, Russia, and the
Dutch East Indies,- are ll in l: ces which the Jana-
nese, in their present mood, consiaer potential enemy
territory. of the three, the orincipal source of Janan's
supply of crude oil at the resent time is the United
States. It is understood that the Japanese Navy Depart-
ment's representative on the interuepartmental com ittee
is opposing the plan for control of the gasoline trade
which is outlined above because his oepertment fears
that its sources of crude oil would be cut off in re-
taliation for the injury that would be done to the
foreign gasoline companies operating in Japan.
The local manager for the Socony Vacuum Corporation,
in conversation with members of the staff of this Embassy,
has expressed the view that his company's position might
be helped by some American governmental action under the
National Recovery Act. He thinks that, in the interest
the

=== Page 174 === (confidence 0.98)
- 7 -
the refineries in the United States, and safeguarding
his company's investments in Jupan, the Japanese might
be required to import a certain amount of gasoline if
they wish to import crude oil. In order to put such
a plan into effect some coop rative :greement would have
to be arrived at with the Shell inter sts who control
the other nearest source of suoply, that in the Dutch
East Indies. Soviet fus ia :noears to be disinclined
to sell crude oil, or, at le st, coulu not be denenued
upon to furnish a steady supply to Japan. Rumania has
been mentioned by the Japanese as a source if the American
supply should be interfered with . The Embassy is not,
however, in a oosition to comment on Rumanian oil.
Respectfully yours,
Joseph C. Grew.

Goold at the latter's office in Yokohana and stated
that he had heard of the Socony's di ficulties with
the Customs at D iren, over the duty to be paiu on
kerosene, the apparent discrimination gainst foreign
companies in the plans to org: uize an oil nonopoly in

Tokyo's despatch No. 701, March 9, 1934.
encloses a translation of the oil control
law and of a memorandum of a conversation
between representatives of American and
British oil companies and the Minister of
Commeroe and Industry with regard to the
operation of the law.
The despatch sets forth the principal
features of the law and the provisions
which will probably be written into the
regulations for the enforcement of the
1aw.
The memorandum of conversation refers
to an assurance given by the Minister that
the law will not be employed to force the
foreign countries out of the Japanese
market, but the Embassy hints that the
assurance may not amount to much.
This despatch does not call for action.
We have authorized the Embassy to
say to the foreign office that we hoped
that American companies would be given the
same treatment as Japanese companies.
The latest telegram on the subject from the
Embassy stated that the American oil
companies preferred to have the statement
withheld until oil control system is put
ihw operation and an oppor unity has been
to observe its workings.

Sir:
I have the honor to refer to my despatches Nos.
844.63632
366, of Apr11 21, 1933, 384, of May 11, 1933, 522, of
54
September 15, 1933, and 562,/of 0ctober 26, 1933, a11
of which deal with the oil problems of Japan and the
APR 2 4 1934
FILED
possibility of some system of governmental control of
the oil industry being inaugurated in this country,
57
and to my telegram No. 39, of March 5, & p.m., stating
. that a government bill giving virtually complete con-
trol of oil importation and refining to a government
commission had been introduced into the Diet.
The

=== Page 187 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
The complete text of the proposed law, as translated
1/. and published by the.JAPAN ADVERTISER, is enclosed here-
with. As will be observed from the text, the legislation
would compel refiners and i norters to obtain licenses to
operate in Japan; would permit the Governmont to reulate
the spheres of activity of the oil comp nies; voulu give
the Government suthority to supervise the oper tions of
refiners and importers; would require Government per-
mission to amalg n te or li uid te oil businesses in Japan;
would limit oil transactions to the licensed firms; would
require refiners and imoorters to maintain cortain minimum
stocks of oil; would recuire refiners nd importers to
sell to the Government at ny time t current prices;
would authorize the Government to set prices at any time
deemed necessary in the public interest; would :uthorize
the Government to recuire refiners and importers to in-
crease or improve cquioment when deemed necessary; would
place the administration of the law in the hands of a
governmental commission; would provide for punishment
of violators of the law by cancelation of licenses; would
recuire refiners nd importers to report on all phases
of their business and to open their books nd records to
inspectors; and would provide various fines for violations.
The date of coming into force of the law is not fixed but
will be determined by Imperial Ordinance, and all refiners
and importers in business at the time of the coming into
force of the law will be considered to be licensed.
The proposed legiolation will, if passed, place very
wide powers in the hands of a governmental commission,
which

=== Page 188 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
which, it is understood in the oil trade, will be
composed exclusively of officials from various govern-
ment departments. In view of the wide powers granted
by the proposed legislation, the detailed regulations
for enforcement of the law will naturally be of great
importance to the oil trade. These regulations have
not as yet been drafted, but it is understood in the
trade that the regulations will cover definitions of
the petroleum products to be includea in the scope of
the law; will establish certain minimum sizes of refineries
to be licensed; will limit the number of licenses so as
not to disturb the balance of supply and demand; will
require annual licenses; will require importers and
refiners to operate continuously or with only short
shut-downs; will limit imports by refiners to their
own needs; will require refiners and iaporters to main-
tain stocks amounting to one-half of the previous year's
imports(thus providing a six-months' supply at all times);
and will require refineries not coming within the fixed
minimum size to shut down after a period of years.
To the foreign (American and British) oil companies
operating in Japan, the primary interest lies in the spirit
in which the law, if passed, will be administered, as
the law can obviously be employed as a means of forcing
the foreign companies out of this field, if such a result
is deemed desirable by the authorities. The question
is whether the law will be used merely as a means of
stabilizing the oil industry of Japan and ensuring an
adequate

=== Page 189 === (confidence 0.98)
-4-
adequate supply of petroleum products at reasonable
prices in time of war, or will be used as a lever for
gradually eliminating the foreign oil firms from the
Japanese trade and placing the entire trade in the hands
of Japanese firms. Ostensibly the law was designed to
fulfill the former purpose, but the possibility lways
exists that it might be used for the l tter purpose.
The two largest foreign oil companies therefore ap-
proached the Ministur of Commerce nd Inuustry on the
subject, and received oral assurances that the v lue of
the foreign oil companies to Japan, as reli ble sup-
pliers of petroleum products, is fully anpreci: tec, and
that the intent of the nev legisl&tion is to protect,
rather than to jeopurdize, the existing business of the
2/
foreign oil companies. A copy of an aiae memcire, com-
piled by the representatives of the oil companies and
containing the complete conversation, is enclosed here-
with. This statement, however, is merely the utterunce
of a present Cabinet Minister, ana cannot in any way be
considered as binding on future aaministrations, or as
determining the attitude of the oil commission toward the
foreign oil companies. It is possible that, in order to
safeguara tneir interests and investments in Janan, the
foreign oil companies will be compelled to incorporate
in Japan, taking in & large proportion of Japanese capital
and thereby securing the political assistance of the
large financial groups.
Although the proposed legisltion would appear to
constitute a radical experiment in state socialism, it
has attracted little attention outside the oil and auto-
mobile trades. This is probably because the legislation
has

=== Page 190 === (confidence 0.99)
-5-
has been brought forward as a national fuel policy, de-
signed to safeguard national defense. The only large news-
paper to comment upon the legislation was the OSAKA MAI-
NICHI, which states, in its English Edition of March 4, 1934,
that "The motive of the act is to moderate the shortage of
oil resources in this country and to orevent a confusion of
oil prices in home markets." However, the editorial further
states, "There is no doubt that by the license system on oil
refining and oil i orts,  general control of our oil in-
dustry will be realized nd  step made toward the firm
establishment of a national policy on oil. However, inasmuch
as the crude oil output in this country is almost negligible,
and a major portion of our oil must always come from abroad...
we shall not be immune from external pressure." In oraer to
remove this "external pressure", the newspaper advises
governmental aid to drilling within Janan, Jap nese invest-
ment in foreign oil fields, the encouragement of production
of industrial alcohol, and the promotion of production of
oil from coal.
Some small opoosition to the proposed legislation has
arisen among the motor-bus and taxicab companies. About
three hundred representatives of such companies throughout
Japan met in Tokyo on March 3, 1934, and passed two resolu-
tions, one condemning the gasoline price agreement among
the six large suppliers, anu the other criticizing the
proposed oil law because it does not establish a funda-
mental remedy for Japan's oil problems and because it would
control imports and thus tend to raise the price of gaso-
line in Japan.
It is not believed that the opposition which has thus
far arisen will be sufficient to prevent or delay passage
of

=== Page 191 === (confidence 0.99)
-6-
of the bill through the Diet, and in the oil trade it is
expected that the bill will be aoproved within the next
few days.
Respectfully yours
Joseph C. Grew.

AIDE MEMOIRE.
MEMORANDUM SUBMITTED BY THE FOREIGN COMPANIES: H1s
Excellency said he agreed in principle with the views
expressed, but he observed that the memorandum gave
no specific reasons as to why the foreign companies dis-
liked the idea of an amalgamation of the Petroleum com-
panies and further said that he read between the lines
that, while they were glad to co-onerate with the N.0.C.,
the foreign comoanies did not wish to co-operate with some
of the other Japanese companies.
Mr. Malcolm explained our osition on the cuestion
of amalgamation, which is s follows:-
1.
Our main objection to & merger of companies is
that we should then be placed in the position of
wholesale crude oil supoliers, and even if we
were given a first option on the supnly we could
never be certain that we should not be under-
quoted by someone holuing a temporary sunply of
cheap oil, and so lose our offtake. Our funda-
mental position is that of producers and as such
we wish to have  secure of take for the products
we refine from our oil by having our own selling
organization. If placed in the position of whole-
sale suppliers we could only regard the market
as an intermittent one which could only be sup-
plied as our production nd stock position in-
dicated, and we shoula be compelled to look else-
where for more regular murkets, such as Jopan is
today.
2.
There would be great difficulty in valuing assets
in an amalgamcted company, particularly as many
of our assets were accuired with a view to the future
and have a future value.
3.
We should lose control of our assets and, in fact,
be leasing them in return for a dividend from the
amalgamated compuny.
4.
As a minority partner our initiative would be cur-
tailed.
5.
In Japan's own interests we believe the erection
of raineries, apart from the cuestion of whther
or not they are fundamentally economic, has many
disadvantages, among them being
(a) It is difficult to balance the manufacture of
the various products with the demand.
(b)
Concentration of supply points means greater
vulnerability in time of emergency.
6.

=== Page 194 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
6. We believe the creation of a semi-official company
might lead to less security of supply in case of
emergency. It would possibly be more difficult in
certain circumstances for a foreign company to
supply a semi-official concern than to supply its
own regular selling organization.
As regards co-operation with Japanese companies, we
are most willin: to co-operate not only with the Nippon
Oil Company but with any Japanese comnuny. The point we
strove to make in our memorandum was that we did not con-
sider it in the interests of the industry to continue to
take artificial steps, such as the giving of cuotas, to
establish new merchants in the business, who were only
middlemen and had no reliable or economic source of supply
behind them. We feel that such a policy would mean that
Ja an would gradually come to rely more and more on casual
and unstable sources of suoply. This would : lso cut down
our share of the trade, rendering our position less cortain
and making it more difficult for us to give the service
and security of supply we are anxious to provide.
His Excellency explained that the object of the amalga-
mation proposal was only to achieve stability: that it was
only one suggested method anu that he personelly did not favour
such a plan.
His Excellency then sked the foreign companies' re-
presentatives to hear his announcement regarding the oil
question: afterwards he would like to hear their opinions:-
He said first that the Minister had seen the memorandum
of February l2th and had asked him to give the foreign com-
panies' representatives the assurance, regarding the proposed
legislation,that the foreign and Japanese companies would
derive equal benefit from official pr tection of their busi-
ness and that there was no underlying principle in the law
aiming at discrimination against or the exclusion of the
foreign companies.
His Excellency went on to say:-
SUPPLY PROBLEM: The Japanese Government realizes that the
country is almost entirely dependent on foreign supplies of
oil and that this situation is not likely to be changed in
the near future. No national policy could be framed without
taking this situation into consideration and there was not
the slightest intention of treating the foreign concerns less
favourably than the Japanese companies.
THE NEW LAW: (a) The Bill would probably establish an
advisory committee to assist the competent Minister and the
committee would consist of officials from various government
departments, including the Foreign Office.
(b) It was probable that the Minister of
Commerce and Industry or his Permanent Vice Minister would
be Chairman of the Committee: it would be his 1dea as far
as

=== Page 195 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
as possible to let the oil companies themselves settle all
questions and, apart from general policy supervision, the
Government would act as arbitrator of differences in opinion
and would have powers to enforce its decision.
LICENSES: The granting of licenses would be settled
by the Minister and his committee.
There was no definite policy to have all the
country's requirements refined in Japan. His Excellency
personally did not consider such a thing oossible. He did
not think that the foreign companies would be asked to re-
fine in Japan the quantity of refined products they are now
importing, but he could not say whether or not, in regard
to the foreign companies' future share of the trade, they
would be asked to refine in Japan the increase b yond their
present volume of trade, but he did not think the Government
would bring pressure to bear on them to do so if it was
uneconomic.
He did not anticipat any alteration in the import
tariff in the immediate future.
If the foreign companies wished to erect refineris
in Japan he thought permits would not be refused provided the
supply and demand position justified the erection of further
refineries. Such refineries would receive equal treatment
with Japanese refineries except that in the event of any
refinery subsidies being granted any company with 50% or more
foreign capital would not qualify for a subsidy.
It was not the intention of the Department
of Industry and Commerce to base licenses on existing unused
refinery capacity but the Government would have to remember
that it is uneconomic to have too much unused refinery
capacity.
Generally in considering licenses and the position
of the various companies the Government would take into ac-
count supply and demand.
GENERAL POSITION: The general objective of the Government
is stability for the industry and in spite of what some people
said regarding severe competition being good for industry
His Excellency did not think it a good thing that merchants
should be able to pick up casual cheap supplies and disturb
the market.
He asked whether the foreign companies could
control the supplies of Crude Oil to Japan and said this
might be one way of stabilizing . The representatives of
the foreign companies said that at the moment, leaving out
of account any dvelopments arising out of the N.R.A., their
Groups could not control the supply of Crude but that world-
wide efforts were being made by the leading companies to
stabilize

=== Page 196 === (confidence 0.99)
-4-
stabilize supply and demand. They felt that Japan was
tonding to rely more and more on less stable sources of
supply and that if the Government would say frankly what
position they wished to attain regarding supply both of
refined products and crude the foreign compinies would be
only too glad to be allowed to do what they could to reach
security of sunply on the lines indicated by the Government.
His xcellency suid that the Bill had been anproved by
the various Government Departments and might come into
effect in April or May. As soon :s the Bill was published
he would be glad to go through the various clauses with the
representatives of the foreign companies.
The representatives of the companies thanked His
Excellency for his kindness in giving them his views which
they feel were intinded to reassure them that they could
continue without hesitation to trade and make the necessary
investments in Japan, points about which they had exnressed
some anxiety in their memorandum.

COPIES SENT TO

O.N.I. AND M. I. D.
Sir:
I have the honor to refer to the/Embassy's telegram
1946362159
No. 39, dated March 5, 2 p.m., to the Department's tele-
graphic reply, No. 28,dated March 13, 7 p.m., and to the
59
s
Embassy's telegram No. 60,dated March 29, noon, all 
FILED
which concern the)new Japanese legislation known as the
19
Petroleum Industry Law and the position of American o
interests in Japan under the new law. I asked that au-
thorization be granted me to make certain oral and inform-
al representations, which authorization was given in the
Department's

=== Page 204 === (confidence 0.99)
I called on the Vice Minister for Foreign Affairs on
April 2, 1934, and stated informally and orally that the
American Embassy had been watching with interest the re-
cent development of a national control of the oil indus-
try in Japan, and was especially interested in the opera-
tion of the new Petroleum In ustry Law just approved by
the Diet. I pointed out that obviously the spirit in
which the operation of this law is c rrie: out is of the
great st importance to Americon oil interests in Japan,
and stated that the Embassy ha? noted that, in introduc-
ing the Petroleum Inaustry Bill in the Lower House of the
Diet, Mr. Kenzo Aoyama, tie Chairman of the Committee which
examined the bill, stated that the primary objectives of
the law were (1) to secure an adequate supply of oil at all
times and (2) to regulate imports of oil with a view to pro-
moting the development of the oil refining industry in Japan.
I said that it was hardly necessary to state that it is this
second objective which is of particular interest to the
American oil interests in Japan.
I stated that the American oil companies operating in
Japan had been for many years cooperating with the Japanese
oil industry to the end that Japan may be assured of a
steady supply of petroleum products at reasonable prices,
and are desirous of continuing this cooperation but are
anxious to know what the future policy of the Japanese
Government toward foreign oil interests, in view of the new
law and its enforcement regulations, is to be, in order that
they may be in a position to formulate their own future
policies and to determine whether further investment and
. installation

=== Page 205 === (confidence 0.97)
- 3 -
installation in Japan will be justified. I said that I
assume that the Japanese Governnent, in regulating the
oil industry, will accord to American oil companies and
refineries in Japan the same rights and privileges as
are or may be granted to Ja anese oil companies and re-
fineries, and that it will give the same consideration
to the interests of the American oil concerns that it
may ive to the interests of Japanese oil concerns.
The Vice Minister for Foreign Affairs said that he
fully appreciuted the position of the Anericun oil com-
panies; that he would look into the matter, and if at
any time he should be in a position to give me informa-
tion concerning the points that I ha raised, he would
be very glad to do so.
151
/ As reported in my telegram No. 60, March 29, noon,
the new Petroleum Inustry Law was a proved by the Diet
and duly promulgated. The full text of the law was pub-
lished in the OFFICIAL GAZETTE on March 28, 1934, and a
1/
translation, made in the Embassy, is enclosed herewith.
It will be observed that the text of the law, as approved
and promulgated, does not differ essentially from the
translation appended to my despatch No. 70l/of March 9,
1934. The various enforcement regulations, to be provided
by Imperial Ordinances, have not yet been promulgated.
The law places very wide powers in the hands of the Govern-
ment, to be administered in some cases with the advice of
an Oil Industry Commission. In the oil trade it is under-
stood that the actual administration of the law will be
in the hands of the Department of Commerce and Industry,
while

=== Page 206 === (confidence 0.99)
- 4 -
while the advisory commission will consist of governmental
officials representing the Army, Navy, Foreign Office
and the two Houses of the Diet.
The law places the foreign oil companies operating
in Japan in a very precarious position, as the law could
easily be used, if cesired, to force them out of the
Japanese trade. In the trace, however, it is believed
that the law will not be used for this urpose, for the
present, at least, but that the foreign oil companies
will be permitted to retain that proportion of the present
Japanese oil trade which they are now handling. All
future increment of the trade, however, it is believed
will be given to Japanese oil companies. The American
oil companies therefore believe that they can look forward
to some years of Japanese business of about the same pro-
portions as their present business, but they do not believe
that they will be able to participate in the future growth
of the oil trade in Japan. The newspapers state that the
existing Japanese oil refining concerns have already filed
applications for licenses to extend their plants, in anti-
cipation of the increased business which they expect will
be allotted to them, while several new concerns have filed
applications to operate. It is probable, however, that
these firms have no immediate intention of erecting re-
fineries, and that the applications were filed primarily
in order to establish claims to participation in future
increases in the trade.
Another factor has arisen during the past few weeks
which

=== Page 207 === (confidence 0.98)
- 5 -
which tends to make still more gloomy the outlook of the
American oil trade in Japan. This is,as briefly reported
in my telegram No. 60 of March 29, noon, the possibility
that a larger proportion of Netherlands Indies crude oil
than has been used in the past will in the future be im-
ported for refining in Japan and Manchuria. In past years
over half of the crude oil used in Japrese refineries has
been imported from the Un:ted States, tio remainder coming
from Russian Saghalien, the Netnerlands Indies and other
sources. The refineries preferred American crude oil, as
a larger proportion of gasoline could be obtained from it
than from other crude oils. In recent months, however,
the Netherlands Indies Government has taken various steps
to check the flooding of the Nethorlands Indies markets
with cheap Japanese goods and has also proposed a conference
to regulate the trade between Japan and the Netherlands
Indies. There is an excess of exports from Japan to the
loL
Netherlands Indies, over the imports from those islands,
of some hundred million yen annually, and the Japanese
Government believes that the Netherlands Indies Government
wishes to place the trade on a barter basis, balancing
imports against exports. If this is done, Japan will be
compelled greatly to increase its purchases from the
Netherlands Indies, or to lose a part of its export trade
to those islands. The Netherlands Indies do not produce
much that can be used in Japan except crude oil. It is
therefore rumored (and the Embassy has especial reason to
believe that the rumors are correct) that the Japanese
Department of Commerce and Industry is planning to propose, if
and

=== Page 208 === (confidence 1.00)
-6-
nd when the negotiations with the Netherlands Indies
Government are opened, that Japan greatly increase its
purchases of Netherlands Indies crude oil in order to
decrease the excess of exports. It is estimated by the
newspapers that Japanese and Manchurian refineries can
consume additional Netherlands Indies oil to the value
of around thirty million yen annually. If the Dutch
proposals for negotiations are accepted (which seems
likely) and the above-outlined plan to purchase the bulk
of the Japanese requirements of crude oil from the Nether-
lands Indies is put into effect, there will be a corres-
ponding decrease in the purchases of American crude oil.
Respectfully yours
Joseph C. Grew.

=== Page 210 === (confidence 0.99)
Law No. 26.
OIL INDUSTRY LAW.
Article 1.
Those who desire to engage in oil re-
fining or in the importation of oil shall obtain
permission of the Government.
The scope of oil refining businesses and of oil
894.6363
im ortation businesses an the necessary provisions in
regard to permits shall be fixed by Imperial Ordinances
to be issued.
Article 2. Oil refiners and importers of oil shall,
in accordance with Government orders to be issued, make
plans for their enterprises and then obtain permission
of the Government. This clause shall also be applied
in case alterations are introduced into the plans.
Article 3.
. In case oil refiners or importers of oil
transfer either the whole or part of their ente-prises
(to other parties), or abandon, or discontinue temporarily
their enterprises, they shall, in accordance with Govern-
ment orders, obtain permission of the Government. This
clause shall also apply in case a company engaged in oil
refining or in oil importation shall amalgamate with other
concerns, or dissolve itself.
Article 4.
Except in cases where oil refiners import
oil necessary for refining purposes, the importation of
oil shall be limited to importers of oil. However, this
clause shall not apply to cases for which special rules
are provided in Imperial Ordinances.
The kinds of oil in the foregoing paragraph shall be
specified

=== Page 211 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
specified by Imperial Ordinance.
Article 5. Oil refiners and importers of oil shall, in
accordance with Imperial Ordinance, always keep in stock
a certain quantity of oil to be calculated on the basis
of the quantity of oil imported by them.
Article 6.
. In case the Government, in accorcance with
Imperial Ordinance, proposes to purchase a quantity of oil
owned by oil refiners or im. orters of oil at a price based
on market prices, the proposal may not bc rejected.
Article 7.
The Government may, if deemed necessary for
the benefit of the public, order oil refiners and importers
of oil to alter prices of oil offer d for sale and to
carry out measures necessary for insuring a supply of oil
and other measures for adjustment between the demand and
supply of oil.
The Government may, if deemed necessary for the benefit
of the public, order oil refiners and importers of oil to
enlarge the scope of, or improve, their equipment.
Article 8. In case the Government grants the permission
mentioned in Article 1 or issues the orders mentioned in
Article 7, the Government shall, except in cases for which
special rules are provided in Imperial Ordinance, obtain
consent of the Oil Industry Committee.
The organization of an Oil Industry Committee shall
be fixed by Imperial Ordinance.
Article 9. In case oil refiners or importers of oil ignore
the provisions of the present law or orders to be issued
under this law, or fail to carry out orders of the Govern-
ment,

=== Page 212 === (confidence 0.99)
- 3 -
ment, the Government may cancel the permission mentioned
in Article 1 or may discharge the officers of a juridical
person or persons.
Article 10. The authorities of administrative offices
may order oil refiners and inporters of oil to submit
reports on conditions of their business, and may issue
other orders and take measures neces ry to supervision.
The authorities of administrative offices may, if
deemed necessary for su ervision, order their officials
to inspect business offices, factories, oil tanks and
other places belonging to oll refiners and importers of
oil, and examine books, documents and other things. In
such cases, officials shall carry documents of identifica-
tion with them.
Article 1l. Those who engage in the oil refining industry
or in the importation of oil without permission of the
Government shall be subjected to a fine of less than ¥5,000.
Article 12. Those who violate the provisions of Article 4
shall be subjected to a fine of less than ¥3,000.
Article 13. Oil refiners and inporters of oil shall be
ordered to pay a fine of less than ¥3,O00 when they come
under the purview of either of the following items:-
(1) Violation of the provisions of Article 5.
(2) Violation of the provisions of Article 7.
Article 14. Oil refiners and importers of oil shall be
ordered to pay a fine of less than ¥1,Oo0 when they violate
the provisions of Articles 2 and 3.
Article 15. Those who come under the purview of either of
the following items shall be subjected to a fine of less
than

=== Page 213 === (confidence 0.99)
-4 -
chan ¥500.
(1) Those who have failed to present reports
stipulated in the first paragraph of Article 10,
or submitted false reports, or violated orders
or measures necessary for supervision.
(2)
Those who have refused, or pr vented, or in-
tentionally :voiled th inspection and examina-
tion by offici ls as provided for in the second
paragraph of Article 10, or who have failed to
reply to questions by officials, or made false
sttements.
Article 16. If representatives, heads of families and
other members of families, and other employees of oil
refiners or importers of oil violate the provisions of the
present law or orders to be issued under this law, in
connection with their business, suc: oil refiners and im-
porters of oil shall not be free fron punishment even if
the violation is not due to their own instructions.
Article 17. In case oil refiners or importers of oil
are juridical persons, punishments to be applied to oil
refiners or importers of oil under the present law or orders
to be issued in accordance with this law, shall only be ap-
plied to managing directors, directors and other officers
who carry on the business of such juridical persons.
Punishments shall also be applied to legal representatives
of oil refiners or importers of oil when they are minors or
incompetent persons. However, this clause shall not apply
in case minors have equal capacity as adults with respect
to the business.

Petroloum Industry Control Law No. 26
On Maroh 27th, 1934 the above law was enacted

Chosen and Karafuto. The principal objeots of the law as we
underetand them are:
(a) The acoumulation by October lst, 1935 of large reserve
supplies (presumably bonded) of Gasoline, Heavy Oil and
Orade Oil, •quivalent to about 6 months sales. This
reserve stock to be in addition to the duty paid supplies

the Japanese Empire, preferably under Japanese capital.
(e) The control (by a system of annual lioenses) of imports -
and in consequence sales - of practically all petroleum
produets.
(d) The control by the Government of minimum and maximum
sellins prices of prectically all petroleum products.

EMBASSY OF THE
UNITED STATES OF AMERICA
Tokyo, August 24, 1934.
No. 938.
Subject:
American Oil Interests in Japan.
yto
5134
livision of
FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
894.6363
NOTED
SEF·10.1934
CONFIDENTIAL
Departmnot of State
894.6363/71
FLLED
The Honorable
The Secretary of State,
Washington.
sir:
I have the honor to refer to the Imbassy's despatch
No. 734,/dated pr1l 5, 1934, on the subjeet of the Petro-
leum Industry Law, then just promulgated, and to report
that the conditions of the Law and its enforcement regula-
tions place such onerous burdens on the foreign oil com-
panies that it is doubtful if they will be able to continue
to operate in Japan. This is due primarily to the stock-
earrying provisions of the Law.
Article

=== Page 251 === (confidence 0.98)
- 2 -
Artiole 5 of the Law (a translation of which was
tranmitted with the Imbassy's despatch No. 734) provi-
des that
"Oil refiners and importers of oil shall, in
accordance with Imperial Ordinance, always
keep in stock a certain quantity of oil to be
calculated on the basis of the quantity of
oil imported by them."
Article 9 of the Law provides that licenses granted to
importers or refiners of oil may be cancelled if the
provisions of the law are not carried out, while Article
13 provides a fine of not over Yen 3000 for violations
of Article 5. The foreign oil companies are thus re-
quired to carry out the stock-carrying provisions of
the Law or subject themselves to fines of Yen 3000 for
each violation or to cancellation of their licenses to
operate in Japan.
The regulations for the enforcement of the Petroleum
Industry Law, promulgated on June 27, 1934, apply the
stock-carrying provision of the Law to gasoline, heavy
oil (i.e., fuel oil and diesel oil) and crude oil, and
require that the amount of stock to be carried shall not
be less than one-half of the quantity imported in the
previous twelve months, the requirement applying to each
kind of oil separately. These stocks are to be carried
in addition to the orainary working stocks, which usually
consist of two or three months' supply of the principal
types of oil sold. The regulations require that by
April 1, 1935, in addition to working supplies, the oil
companies must have three months' stocks in storage, and
by Ootober 1, 1935, six months' stocks in storage.
The stook-carrying provisions of the Petroleum In-
dustry Law, if carried into effeet, will require large
investments

=== Page 252 === (confidence 0.99)
- 3 -
investments in Japan by the American and British oil
companies, not only for the purchase of land and the
construction of additional storage facilities, but also
for the stooks of oil. The Rising Sun Petroleum Company
(British Shell interests) estimates that it will be com-
pelled to expend eventually around thirty million yen for
tanks and oil. The Standard-Vacuum Oil Company estimates
that its expenditures will amount only to about ten million
yen altogether, as it already possesses greater storage
facilities than does the Rising Sun Petroleum Company.
These investments will have to be made before October 1,
1935, or (presumably) the two companies will be compelled
to cease to handle the types of oil products of which
stocks are required. The two companies therefore have
little time in which to arrive at a decision in regard to
the investments.
The Petroleum Industry Law also requires the refiners
and importers of petroleum and petroleum produots to sell
their supplies to the Japanese Government at any time at
such a price as the Government may consider to be current;
to expand or improve their equipment as ordered by the
Government; to sell their products at prices fixed by
the Government; to carry out fully any annual sales or
import plans which have been submitted to the Government
for approval and which have been approved; and to open
their books and places or business, storage plants, dis-
tributing plants, etc., to Govermment inspection at any
time. Anmual licenses to import and sell petroleum pro-
ducts must also be obtained; these licenses can be used to
diminish or entirely stop the business of the foreign oil
companies

=== Page 253 === (confidence 0.99)
-4-
companies at any time. For these reasons the foreign
oil companies fedl that their Japan business is most
inseoure and that the power of decision has been taken
from them and placed in the hands of governmental agen-
cies. Under the circumstances they cannot see their
way clear to investing further large sums in Japan and
have addressed letters to the Minister of Gommerce and
Industry, requesting answers in writing to ten questions.
These questions asked for information on the following
subjects:
1. will the same rights, privileges and consider-
ation be accorded to foreign companies as to Japanese
companies?
2. Will the Japanese Government give any guarantee
as to future business if the oil companies invest large
sums for storage facilities?
3. Will it be necessary for the foreign companies,
in order to retain their share of present and future
business, to establish refineries in Japan, either with
entirely foreign capital or in combination with Japanese
capital?
4. will the present tarirf regulations be revised
to permit re-erportation of petroleum products refined
in Japan with a drawback on the crude oil imported?
5. Will the Japanese Government compensate the
foreign oil companies for equipment rendered useless,
in case they find themselves unable to invest further
large sums in Japan and their business suffers in con-
sequence?
6. Is the Japanese Govermment considering
a.

=== Page 254 === (confidence 0.98)
-5 -
a.
Any change in the import tarirf on oils
b.
The introduction of subsidies, or
0.
Any other measures which should be taken
into consideration by the foreign oil
companies when formulating their future
plans?
7.
Will there be any assurance that the regulations
requiring that six months' stocks be carried will not be
changed and larger stocks required?
8. Is the question of reducing the stock-carrying
requirements of the Law being considered by the Government?
9. Does the Japanese Government intend to shoulder
the expenditure for land, tanks, equipment and oil stored,
and the risk of loss from fire, earthquake, leakage, and
evaporation involved in the provisions of the Law or are
the expenditures and risks to be borne by the foreign
oil companies?
10. In fixing selling prices for petroleum pro-
ducts in Japan, will the Government take into considera-
tion the greater costs of the companies importing refined
products, due to the import tariff?
No reply has as yet been received by the foreign oil
companies to their representations.
The foreign oil companies also claim that the new
law is inequitable and unjust, in that it is retroactive
upon the past investments of the companies. That is,
unless the new provisions requiring the storage of large
stocks of oil are carried out, the companies will be
unable to continue in business in Japan and their past
investments in storage facilities, distributing plants,
tank cars and lighters, office buildings, etc., will
become useless and will lose the greater part of their
value.
The

=== Page 255 === (confidence 0.98)
- 6 -
The complaints of the foreign oil companies regard-
ing the provisions of the Petroleum Industry Law have been
1/
embodied in a memorandum, a copy of which is enolosed here-
with.
It is improbable that the foreign oil companies will
consent to the requirements of the new Law in regard to
the carrying of stocks. The Standard-Vacuum oil Company
in New York has telegraphed to its local representative,
stating that if the Japanese Government insists on the
fulfillment of the stock-carrying provisions of the Law,
it will expect the Japanese Government to provide the
storage facilities and to assume the risks of loss through
fire, earthquakes, leakage, evaporation, etc. It further
asserts that the conditions required in Japan by the Law
are the most onerous of any country in the world, and
states its belief that if the foreign oil companies firmly
refuse to carry out the stock-keeping requirements, the
Japanese Government will eventually modify or abandon
its demands. (The Embassy doubts that the Japanese
Government can be induced to abandon its stook-carrying
demands, as the demands are based on the requirements of
national defense. However, in its passage through the
Diet, a rider was attached to the Petroleum Industry Law,
demanding that the Law should not operate to interfere
with the supplies of oil to Japan. It is possible that
the provisions of this rider could be invoked to force
some modification of the stock-carrying requirements,
if it is found that the two large foreign companies
refuse to carry the stocks required and consequently
cease to handle the oils in question.)
The attitude
of the

=== Page 256 === (confidence 0.96)
-7 -
of the Rising Sun Fetroleum Gompany is understood to be
much the same as that of the Standard-Vacuum Oil Company,
except that the Rising Sun Petroleum Company might con-
sent to the stook-carrying requirements if a definite
guarantee of a minimum annual business for a period of
twenty years or more is given by the Japanese Government.
It is understood that this was done in France when a
governmental oil monopoly was established there. The
Japanese oil companies are also reported to be unwilling
to provide the stock-carrying facilities required by the
Law, but they hope to be able to obtain government funds
or subsidies to assist them in the undertaking,
It is probable that the Department will be approached
by the Stamdard-Vacuum Oil Gompany with a request that the
Embassy in Tokyo take this matter up with the Foreign
Ofrice. In this connection, the Department's attention
is invited to my telegram No. 182 August 20, 3 p.m., in
whioh the possibility of retaliatory measures was discussed.
2/
In this connection I also enclose for the Department's
assistance a compilation of documents relating to the
Petroleum Industry Control Law furnished the Embassy by
the General Manager of the Standard-Vacuum Company in Japan.
Respectrully yours,
Joseph c. Grew. (

HARDBHIPS EXPERIENCED BY THE FORKIGN
COPANIB UNDR THE NE IA.
(A) DEFINITE:
I.
We must either invest a large sum, which is
admitted to be in the interests of the Govern-
ment and/or the Nation and not in our own
interests or necessary to our trade, or else
lose the usefulness of the major part of our
past investments.
II.
There is no security beyond one year for our
new investments.
III.
We do not know whether we shall be allowed to
build a Refinery in spite of the fact that
tariffs are in favor of local refining.
IV.
The Government controls prices and beyond
the fact that it says the Control Law will
lead to such economies as will reimburse the
Companies for their investment in stock-
keeping, we do not know concretely what steps
the Government will take to bring this about,
particularly as we are told that competition
will not be eliminated. We cannot see, there-
fore, that the Government has made any provi-
sion to enable us to amortise our investments.
V.
We can get no assurances of participation in
subsidies if granted.
VI.
We are given too short a time to consider this
question and discuss it with out home office.
一)
VII.
We do not know whether similar or more strin-
gent regulations will be imposed in future.
VIII.
The Government fixes prices and can compel
2
us to sell our stocks and to continue to import
0
and sell according to our Import Programme at a
1~
price fixed by itself. We have been unable to
7
learn from the Government on what basis it
proposes to fix prices.
(B)
PROBABLE:
I.
The Government is supposed to intend to curtail
our share of the trade and give increased shares
to Japanese Companies.
Even if we were to admit that certain ad-
justments might be reasonable, it is essential
that the Government tell us what we have to
expect, if they force us to invest further
large sums to protect our past investments,
and ir there is a radieal ourtailment in our
trade, we should probably have a just complaint.
Ir an organization is made to stand still
it will disintegrate, and we cannot agree to
being forced into the position of a second-
rate Oil Company in Japan.
II.

=== Page 258 === (confidence 0.93)
- 8-
Ⅱ.
It is probable that the Japanese Government
wishes its oil imports to be carried in
Japanese tankers. This may not constitute
a legitimate commercial grievance but it is
a point of considerable importance to our
Nation.
III.
The stocks are required for military purposes.
This again is important, though it may not be
a commercial grievance.

OHDINANCE NO. 196 FOR THE ENFORCEBGENT OF
ME OIL INDUSTRY LAW. PROMILGATED JWE 27, 1934
Art. 1.
The oil refining business referred to in Art. 1 of the
Oil Industry Law refers to the business of making
gasolime, illuminating oil, light oil, mmchine oil or
heavy eil of a mineral mature from material eil af
mimeral neture (inclusive ef cembestible matwra) eas
having clese relations with oil bearing stratum), but
dees not cover the business of making gaselime,
illumimating oil, light oil, mmchine oil or heavy oil
from mterial oil mnufactured from coal or oil mhale,
The oil importing business referred to in Art. 1 of the
Oil Industry Law refers to the business of importing
gasoline, illuminating oil, light oil, mmchine oil,
or crude oil, but does not cover the busimess of
importing illumimating oil of mineral nature, light
oil or maahine vil in tins or barrels or similar
packages exclnsively.
Art. 2.
The permit for the oil refining bus iness referred to in
Art 1 of the Oil Industry Law shall be necessary for
each of the oil refineries.
An oil refinery engaging in the refining of imported
material oil shall be equipped with a straight rum
plant of a refining capacity of not less than 50, 000
kilo-litres per year and a cracking plant of a capaci ty
of not less thmn 25,000 rilo-litres per yoar or a cracking
plant of & capacity of not less than 50,000 kilo-litres
per year.
Art。 3.
The Minister of Commerce and Industry may not grant a
permit for the businezs of refining or importing cil
reforred to in Art. 1 of the Oil Industry Law, then he.
taking into consideration the conditions of the demand
for and the supply of oil, considers the grant of the
permit likely to hinder the healthy development of the
oil industry.
Art.4.
The kinds of oil which none but oil importers can import
because of the provision of Art 4 of the Oil Industry
Law are gasoline, illuminating oil, light oil, machine
oil, hoavy oil and crude oil of a mineral nature. Those
importable under the mme Article by oil refiners are
beavy oil and crude oil of mineral na ture.
Art。5.
In the came of importing oil failing mder any of the
following items, the importer need not be a regular oil
imperter:
I. Oil to be imported for the Government.
2. Oil for sample er specimen purposes or oil being
carried by travellers.

Japanese Petroleum Monopoly and
Related Developments and Problems.
894.6363/73
SEP 12 1934
LIVISION OF
Mr. Molekamp called on Mr. Hamilton and was brought
by Mr. Hamilton to Mr. Hornbeok.
Mr. Molekamp stated that he had received from his
Government, on Saturday afternoon, September 8, a telegram
whioh stated that the Netherland Government had instructed
its Minister in Tokyo to approach the Minister for Foreign
Affairs and in a friendly manner point out to the latter
that the Japanese petroleum regulations injuriously affected
Netherland interests and to request that these regulations
be altered. The features which he was to stress as the
most objectionable were (a) the requirement for six months'
storage purchasable at a price set by the Japanese Govern-
ment, (b) the provision that the quotas (of import) may be
changed annually by the Japanese Government, and (c) the
fact that the Japanese Government expects to give preference
ELED
t
F

=== Page 321 === (confidence 0.96)
- 2 -
to orude oil -- whioh preference would result in injury
to the business of refining outside of Japan.
Mr. Hornbeok inquired whether the instruotion contained
anything in the way of a suggestion for action by other
governments. Mr. Molekamp replied that it did not, but he
added that it might in itselr be taken as implying a sug-
gestion.
Mr. Hornbeok inquired whether the Netherland Minister
at Tokyo had as yet carried out the instruction. Mr.
Molekamp replied that he was not informed.
Mr. Hornbeck said that we might consider that the
status of the matter at this moment is that the Netherland
Government has informed us of an instruction which it has
given to the Netherland Minister for an approach to the
Japanese Government; that all that we can say at the moment
is that we appreciate being given this information and we
will give the matter consideration. Mr. Molekamp requested
that we inform him of any action which we take, if and when.
Mr. Hornbeck and Mr. Hamilton said that we would of course
wish to do so.
In oonolusion, Mr. Hornbeck inquired whether the
Netherland Legation has been informed of any action having
been taken by the British Government. Mr. Molekamp replied
that it had not.
With a repetition by Mr. Molekamp of a request that we
keep him informed, the conversation ended.
(Sum mext tay2).

would be affected by the Japanese regulations are greater
than American interests, and Mr. Hornbeck and Mr. Hamilton
said that they also so understood.

DIVISION OF
Mr. Teagle called me on the telephone from New York.
He said that he had been informed by Sir Henri Deterding
(from Europe) that the Netherland and the British Govern-
ments had made protests to the Japanese Government. He
wondered what information we had and what action we had
taken.
I replied that we had not been informed that those
Governments or either one of them had protested; that we
had had an inquiry from the British Government with regard
to our attitude -- which inquiry had come before Sir Henri
Deterding had reached London, and that the Counselor of
the Netherland Legation had called and had asked us what
was our attitude; but that in neither case had we been in-
formed what the other Government thus inquiring intended
to do or what it proposed, if anything, by way of joint
FILED
action; that we had given the reply in each case that we
would expect the initiative by way of proposals, if any,
to

Mr. Teagle repeated that he had been informed that
they bad protested; and he said that he would cheok back
ezd would expeot to have further information on Monday
next.

DIVIBION OF
Mr. Teagle called me on the telephone from New York
and said that he had been informed definitely by Sir Henri
Deterding's representative in New York that the Netherland
and the British Governments had protested to the Japanese
Government. He would like to know whether we had been
informed.
I stated that we had not been so informed; that we
had had nothing since the telephone conversation between
Mr. Teagle and me on Friday last; but that the Commeroial
Attaché of the Netherland Legation was coming in to see us
in a few minutes and it might be assumed that he would
have something to tell us.
Mr. Teagle said that probably the Commercial Attaché
would give us information corresponding to that which he,
Mr. Teagle, had; and that he would call me up again this
FILED
afternoon.
F
八

=== Page 326 === (confidence 0.95)
- 2 -
Mr. Teagle called me this afternoon on the telephone
from New York and I informed him in confidenoe and briefly
with regard to the information given us this morning by
the Commercial Attaché of the Netherland Legation (see
773
memorandum of today's date). He asked whether there was
anything that he could do at this stage. He said he hoped
that we would make a protest. I said that it should be
understood that the instructions of the Netherland Govern-
ment to its Minister in Tokyo, as reported to us, did not
use the. word "protest" and seemed to envisage friendly
representations. I said that if the representations are
made seriatim and without the appearance of concerted action,
it might be that the effect would be somewhat weakened. I
said that we were expecting to hear something from London
in the near future; and that in the interval I did not see
that there was anything which we could do at this moment.
It was agreed that we would keep in touch -- and the con-
versation there ended.

DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
September 26, 1934.
Tokyo's despatch lNo. 957 of September 6,
1934, in regard to the Japanese petroleum
industry law:
There is transmitted with this despatch
a translation of pertinent excerpts from the
proceedings of the Committee of the Diet to
which the draft of the law was referred last
session. The despatch sets forth important
statements made by Goverrment officials which
have direct bearing on the position of the
foreign oil companies. I suggest that you
read marked portions of the report on the
proceedings of the Diet Committee for further
information in regard to the following points:
1. Sources of Supply. Most of the
crude is obtained from the United States but
it may be possible to obtain crude also from
Borneo, Mexico and Venezuela. One concern
"has been negotiating with a view to getting
hold of petroleum resources in Mexico and
Venezuela"(page 2). Freight costs render
difficult purchases of oil from Rumania (page 7)
2. Protecting Japanese Consumers of
Gasoline by Encouraging Refining In Japan.
It is charged that companies with world-wide
markets are able to sell gasoline below cost
in

=== Page 338 === (confidence 0.99)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
- 2 -
in countries where there is competition
from domestic refineries and to offset losses
in those countries by charging higher prices
in countries which do not produce gasoline.
For example, the price per gallon of gasoline
1s 36 Sen to 38 Sen in California and 36 Sen
in Japan, but it is 75 Sen at Shanghai
(pages 2, 3 and 14).
3. Possibility of Retaliatlon
by Foreign Oi1 Companies (Pages 6 to 8).
4. Quotas for Foreign Companies.
It is not the intention of the Japanese
Government for the present to curtail the
"sales fields" of the foreign oil companies.
(Pages 14 to 17). The Minister of Commerce
and Industry admits, however, that this is a
very delicate matter and one which he cannot
discuss in detail. His statements, however,
underscore "at present", the implication
being that the quotas of the foreign companies
will be reduced as soon as circumstances
permit. You will, of course, have noted on
pages 6 and 7 of the despatch, that the
Rising Sun has been granted a quota equivalent
to the volume handled by it at present, but
that the quota of the Standard Oil has been
reduced owing to a "clerical error", which
the Government would not correct.
5. Maintenance.

=== Page 339 === (confidence 0.98)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
-3 -
5. Maintenance of Stocks. I think
you might read this entire section, pages 22
to 25. You will note that if the companies
are required to keep more than six-months'
stock on hand the Government would feel
obligated to bear the additional cost. The
Government calculates, however, that the cost
of maintaining stocks under the present plan
is less than one Sen per gallon, and it was
believed that this cost would be at least
offset by economies (?) which the law would
enable the companies to effect.
I have not as yet received despatch
No. 956 of September 6, 1934, which 1s
referred to as transmitting a memorandum
of conversation between Neville and the
Vice-Minister for Foreign Affairs.
FE:PWDLY
894.6363/78

Department of Gommerce and Industry before a meeting of
the Fuel Association on July 14, 1954.
The collection of excerpts from the hearings before
the committee of the Diet appointed to examine the Petro-
Leum Industry Law was compiled by the Rising Sun Petroleum
Compamy (Shell interests) and the Standard-Vacuum oil
Company and consists of significant statements prinoipally
made by the Minister for Commerce and Industry, divided
into the following categories: Attitude toward Foreign
Companies; Objects of the Law; quotas for Foreign com-
panies; Priee Gontrol; Stock-keeping (costs); Stoek-
keeping (Loealities); Stoek-keeping (Miscaloulations);
malgamation; Small Refineries; Subsidies; Refining
Capaeity; Tankers; Oil for Ships; and Exports.
From the exeerpts contained in the first sectiom of
the compilation it will be obserred that the questiom of
a possible embargo on the sales of erude oil to Japanese
refineries had attrasted the attention of the Committee
ané was considered even before the passage of the bill
(the hearings were held in February, 1954). Thus, the
minister

=== Page 342 === (confidence 0.98)
-3 -
Minister for Commeree and Industry stated (page 1 of
the exeerpts):
"....it will not do for us to be dependent solely
on these eompanies. In the U.S.A. there are
some sourees or supply not conneeted with the
Standard Oil; also it appears that there are
good sourees of supply in the South Sea Islands
not under the control of those two companies".
Later, however, he decried the possibility that an em-
bargo might be imposed by the United States on the export
of orude oil to Japan (page ?):
*...ir the Japan market is so important for the
big companies that their possession of it is
vital to their existence, they they might adopt
desperate means of acquiring it; actually,
however, the Japan market is quite insignificant
in their eyes in comparison with business in other
countries, and therefore it is out of the question
that they would resort to drastic measures and
expend vital energy in suppressing Japan."
But in a later statement he admitted the possibility that
such an embargo might be imposed (page 8):
"Should the U.S.A. take such an action as to pro-
hibit the export of crude oil to Japan, there
would be nothing less than an economic war, in
which event we should have no alternative but to
look to other countries for supplies."
The Minister and the Gommittee apparently did not take
into consideration at that time the added effeet of the
proposed oil sales monopoly in Manchuria upon American
opinion. The effect of the Petroloum Industry Law upon
American investments in the oil distributing busimess in
Japan only was disoussed.
