The objeets and procedure of the Petroleum Industry
Lam are further explained by Mr. Sakai (one of the perma-
nent officials of the Department of Gommeree and Industry)
in his lecture of July 14, 1934, (Enclosure No. 2). The
greater part of the lecture is concerned with the machinery
of control of the importing and refining of oil, and is
of interest at the moment only as a matter of record.
In the first part of the lecture, however, Mr. Sakai im-
plies that one of the primary purposes of the Law is
eventually to abolish imports of petroleum products into
Japen. He states:
*Our oil industry, nevertheless, still remaims
largely controlled by foreign capital. Over
ome halr of the demands for gasoline and heavy
oil in the country is being supplied by foreign
oil interests.
I hear that our oil refiners are obliged to
Leave about one half of their capasity idle - a
very deplorable phemomenom. It is of utmost
impertanee that they should be enabled to sive
full play to their capacity so that our country
may

=== Page 344 === (confidence 0.98)
-5-
may be rendered self-contained in all petroloum
produots eventually.
To this end, we must regulate the imports of
foreign products which are making their way into
our country....."
In regard to that part of the Petroloum Industry Law
requiring importers and refiners to maintain six months'
stocks in Japan, Mr. Sakai states:
"Stock holaing duty represents another significant
aspect of the Oil Industry Law. It being impossible
to bring about a speedy inorease in the production
of petroleum by hunting for further oil resourees
in our country or by developing the fuel substitute
Industry, we must rescrt to some expedient for main-
taining an adequate supply of petroleum at all times
against emergencies; hence this duty."
The injustice, however, of requiring foreign firms to carry
a part of Japan's war-time reserves of oils does not seem
to have occurred to Mr. Sakai.
Recent Developments.
As was stated in the Embassy's despatch No. 938, the
two large foreign oil companies in Japan (the Standard-
Vacuum Oil Company and the Rising Sun Petroloum Company)
addressed Letters to the Minister for Gommeroe and In-
dustry, requesting answers in writing to ten questions
conserning the future business of the foreign companies
in Japan. Under date of August 31, 1934, Mr. T. Fukuda,
the Chief of the Mining Bureau of the Department of Commerce
and Industry, replied to the letters. ▲ translation of
the reply to the letter from the Standard-Vacuum oil com-
pamy is enalosed herewitk. As will be observed, the
Chief of the Mining Bureau carefully refrained from com-
mitting himself or the Japanese Government in any way,
confiming his written replies to generalities and requesting
a represemtative of the Company to eall and diseuss the
guestions with the officials conserned. Perhaps the most
signirieant

=== Page 345 === (confidence 0.97)
- 6 -
signifieant statement contaimed in the letter is that
"jusimess plans (of the importers of.petroloum
produets) shall be sanctioned taking demand and
supply or petroleum and other things into com-
sideratiom in order to make our petroleum in-
dustry more prosperous. ....Unless something
extraordinary takes place, the quantity to be
sold will not become less than the quantity
sold at the time of the enforcement of the Petro-
leum Industry Law.* (Italics not in original
letter).
The assuranees given in the reply do not by any means
satisry the foreign oil companies, as the assurances are
qualiried by such phrases as "and other things" and
"unless something extraordinary takes place". Moreover,
the reply does not assure the foreign ccmpanies that they
will be allowed to retain their present relative positions
vis-a-vis the Japanese refining companies, but implies,
on the other hand, that they will not be permitted to
share in the natural increase in the petroleum trade and
will only be permitted, at best, to retain the same
quantity of imports as they now handle. The foreign
oil companies do not intend, for the present at least,
to accept the invitation of the Ohief of the Mining
Bureau to discuss the matters presented im their ques-
tionnaires with minor officials of the Department of
Commerce and Iadustry, as they expect that the replies
given will either be evasive or, if specifie, will be
given as the personal opinions of the officials and will
not be binding. The oil companies have not yet decided
upon their mext step in this connectiom.

Reference:
D.4 1/2
MINISTER:..."As you have just said, the greater
part of the imports are brought in by the Rising
Sun and the Standard oil and it will not do for us
to be dependent solely on those companies. In
the U.S.A. there are some sources of supply not
connected with the Standard oil: also it appears
that there are good sources of supply in the south
Sea Islands not under the control of those two
companies. However, this question should be
taken into consideration in connection with our
general trade position, since imports from Japan
are being restricted in the Dutch East Indiea, and,
from that aspect, we have to consider what commodi-
ties should be imported from the D.E.I. to set
off against our exports."

by refiners in Japan is brought from sources not
associated with either the Rising Sun or the
Standard oil.
Californian Crude Oil is obtain-
able from quite a number of concerns not associated
with the Standard oil.
As you probably know,
production in the U.S.A. is so big that a company
or an oilfield which does not count for much from
the American standpoint is quite big when viewed
in the light of consumption in Japan. The
Kettleman Hill Fields, from which Japan is getting
supplies

=== Page 349 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
supplies at present, are situated midway between
San Francisco and Los Angeles. Production there
is being considerably curtailed and is running
to about one-tenth of normal capacity. According
to experts, if one or two of the wells there were
opened up fully, the resultant produotion would
be more than sufficient to meet the entire demand
in Japan. At present Crude Oil is coming mainly
from American sources not connected with the
Standard Oil and I believe that there is a reason-
able chance of our being able to obtain future
supplies from such sources. Moreover, as the
Minister said before, it is not impossible for us
to get Crude Oil from Dutch Borneo, Mexico, Vene-
zuela, etc. as well as from the U.S.A."

D.4 17/18
MINISTER: ... "A big concern manufacturing
Gasoline on a large scale may not make a loss
even if it sells cheaply in Japan: it can sell
high elsewhere and it may have practically a
controlling influence in fixing prices. we may
be right in thinking that so much pressure is
being brought to bear on Japanese refiners that
they are not able to utilize their refining

high1y

=== Page 350 === (confidence 0.99)

highly necessary for us to organize and con-

unfair oppression."
"Big foreign Petroleum companies, which have
the lion's share of the business in markets all
over the world, can sell their products cheaply
in Japan to the detriment of Japanese refiners
because they can get higher prices elsewhere.
The word "dumping" may be out of place but it
is certain that such big companies are able to
control prices and so meet competition. If
unfair competition is eliminated by organization
and coneolidation under the aegis of the proposed
Law, Japanese refiners will be able to develop
their business.
The refining industry in Japan
must be firmly established to a certain degree,
for if we were always to be entirely dependent
on imported products our position would be un-
sound from the standpoint of national defence
and home industry. In fact, the proposed Law
has been drafted with the objective of organizing
and consolidating the refining industry in Japan."
D.4 19/20
MINISTER: ... "Gasoline is sold in San Francisco,
for example, at a price equivalent to 39 sen
per A.G. and in Los Angeles at 38 sen: in Japan
it is sold at 36 Sen. 36 Sen may not be the
price at which both the Rising Sun and the
standard

=== Page 351 === (confidence 0.98)
- 4 -
standard oil are willing to sell of their own
volition. Do you think they would keep on
selling at a low price if they could crush the
Japanese refining industry either by continued
pressure or by a knock down blow?
NO! In
countries where there are no big refineries, in
China, for example, they sell high, and in Japan
they sell low. If the time came that the Japanese
refiners were down and out, the Rising Sun and the
Standard Oil would put up their prices in Japan also.
Therefore the organization and consolidation of the
Japanese refining industry is imperative, in my
opinion, in the lasting interests of consumers.
In point of fact, Gasoline is selling in Shanghai
at 75 Sen - more than double the price current in
Japan, and if it were not for the refiners in Japan,
it would be possible for the foreign companies to
sell at 75 Sen here. The objective of the Petro-
leum Industry Law is the consolidation of the In-
dustry in Japan in order to obviate worldwide manipu-
lations whereby consumers in Japan may profit tem-
porarily at the expense and ultimate ruin of Japanese
refiners and later, after the Japanese refiners are
out of the picture, may have to pay high prices.
Such a situation would be exceedingly disadvantageous
to consumers in the long run. In order to guard
against such a contingency we must organize and
consolidate our Petroleum industry and, in fact,
this is the underlying purpose of the Bill that is
before you.
"It is

=== Page 352 === (confidence 0.99)
-5-
"It is possible that for a time prices may be
high, but it is clear, I think, that Japanese re-
finers will be enabled to survive and that the con-
suming public of Japan will profit thereby in the
long run."

OBJECTS OF THE LAW:

D.2 1/2
DELEGATE: "In view of the present condition of
the Petroleum Industry in this country, and with
the objective of achieving a well regulated
development of the domestic Refining Industry by
means of some suitable regulation of the importa-
tion of Petroleum, it is proposed by this Bill
to make the importation and the refining of Petro-
leum subject to Government Licence; also, in
consideration of the supply and demand position,
to stipulate that those engaged in the Petroleum
Business maintain always stocks of a certain size,
in order to secure prompt and satisfactory supplies.
"These two proposals are the backbone of the
Bill."

D.5 2/3
MEMBER: ... "Will it be the Government's policy

Products in addition to having Crude Oil imported
and refined in Japan?

intend to allow, as at present, the importation of

MINISTER:

provide security for the Petroleum Refining Indus-
try, and, if possible, we should like to have as

their full capacity: further, I consider it desir-
able that Refining Plants and facilities should be
made substantial, and should be improved and ex-
tended.
Nevertheless, we have no intention of
putting any drastic check on the importation of
Refined Products.
As you know, the demand for
Oil - particularly for Refined Products - is very
noticeably increasing, and it will be very difficult
in the immediate future to manufacture in Japan
all the Refined Products required.
Moreover,
there are certain circumstances which necessitate
our allowing the continuation of importation of
Refined Products in order to safeguard the im-
portation of Crude: consequently, it would be
inadvisable suddenly to bring pressure to bear
against the importation of Refined Products.
This

=== Page 362 === (confidence 0.99)
-15-
This is rather a delieate matter, and I do not
wish to make any definite or detailed statement;
I hope you will appreciate my position. In any
case, we have no intention at present of bringing
any pressure to bear against the importation of
Refined Products, or of putting any drastic check
on it. ...."

Re the 011 Dadnstry Lam.
(Lecture by Mr. Eiaki sakai, seeretary of the
Ordinary Meeting of fuel Association held on
July 14th, 1954.)
I am going to explain the main purposes of the oil
Industry Law and express my personal opinions thereon.
This law is one aspect of the so-called national fuel
policy. Its most important object is the control and
maintenance of our oil industry.
In view of the limited oil resources in our country
and of a steadily inoreasing demand for all sorts of
petroleum produets, we must adopt such an oil policy as
aims at an effeetive and speedy development of the oil
resources, stimulus of the oil substitute industry, im-
provement of the oil refining industry, ete. our oil
refining industry has made steady progress in recent
years. In l955, the total output of gasoline in our
country filled 45% or the total requirements for it,
that of illuminating oil 45%, that of machime oil 90%
and that of light oil supplied virtually the entire re-
quirememts, but the production of heavy oil alone was
still so limited that the greater part of the requirements
had to be satisfied by imported products. Compared with
the ammal outputs ten years before (1.e. 19st), the pro-
dnetion of gasoline in 1933 inereased 14 fold and that of
heavy eil 5 fold. The shovings reflested tremendous pro-
gress mmde by our eil refining industry.
Oar cil indastry, mevertheless, still remains largely
cemtrelled by foreign eapital. ever one half ef the
femends far gasoline and heavy oil in the country is being
supplies

=== Page 387 === (confidence 0.97)
- 8 -
sapplied by foreign eil interests.
I heer that our oil refiners are obliged to leave
about ome half of their capasity idle - a very deplorable
phenomenon. It is of urgent importance that they should
be enabled to give full play to their capacity so that our
country may be rendered self-contained in all petroleum
produets eventually.
To this end, we must regulate the imports of foreign
products which are making their way into our country,
sealing over a fairly high tariff-wall. However, there
is danger of too many oil refining companies springing up
as & result. Some sort of State control is therefore
mesessary: hence the permit system provided for in the
Law, which aims at stimulating the import of crude oil
in place of refined produets and fostering our refining
industry to such an extent as it may stand against foreign
refixed oil produets in time.
The produetiom of crude oil in our country is not
mearly emough to cope with the rapidly growing demand for
it. In order to develop our oil refining industry, we
must be able to obtain an adequate supply of fereige craãe
oil without interruptiom, and on the other hand asquire
many more oil consessions abroad. It is likely that an
attempt to develop the oil refining industry without these
requisites will prove a futile effort.
Stoek holding duty represemts another significant
aspeet ef the oil Industry Law. It being impossible to
bring about a speedy iserease in the produetion of petroloum
by hunting for further oil resourees in our country or by
develeping the fuel sabetitute industry, we mmst resort to

=== Page 388 === (confidence 0.96)
-3-
seme expedient for mintaixing an adequate supply of
petroloum at all times against emergencies; hemse this
daty.
The provisions of the oil Dadustry Law may be
roughly grouped under the following four items:
ing and oil import buainesses.
8.
matters regarding rational conduet of oil refin-
ing and oil import busimesses.
3.

Ro. No. 1 item:
The oil refining business requiring & permit (licence)
comprises the production of gasoline, light oil, maehine
oll and heavy oil as well as the collection of gasoline
from natural gas and the so-called topping. It does
not include low temperature coal carbonization, coal-
liquefaction and extraction of oil from shale, which the
Government intends to foster as a distinct industry.
Each refinery is required to have equipment of a
certain type and capacity, inamuch as it is necessary
from an economic stanépoint as well as control standpoint
of view to have all refineries adequately equipped. The
Lew spesiries the installatiom of ome straight-run plant
of a capacity of 50,000 kilolitres or more a year as well
as one cracking plant of a eapacity of 25,000 kilolitres
a year, er alternatively one eracking plant of a eapasity

any other package) alone, inammuch as the imports of
petroleum in packages are comparatively mall at present,
and the exclusion of those who exclusively import the same
from the category of oil-importers is not likely to inter-
fere with the realization of the purposes of the Law.
While, in prineiple, oil-importers alone are allowed
to import oil, refiners are exeeptionally allowed to im-

It is not proper that a sudden change should, through

it will jeopardize the stability of the oil market.

At the time the bill for this law was first introduced
in the Biet, many arguments were set forth. one of the
most worth-listening to was that development of oil-
substitutes should be carried on energetisally in addition
to an effective exploitation of oil resources. Another
was that care should be taken to prevent the law being
operated in suoh a manner as will benefit the oil companies
alone regardless of the interests of the consuming public.
An economic policy based upon free competition is by
no means suited for the present times. society is not a
casual, disorderly congregation of individuals, but an
organic (aystematized) combimation of individuals. A
community will not effect a healthy developmemt unless
eash of its members acts always with its interests at heart.
The same holds good with regard to the mational economy.

should be placed under a judicious contrel or guidance
of the central organ of the country concermed.
The Oil Ineustry Law represents one of the various
characteristic features of the so-called control economy.
At any rate, what effect the law will have upon the oil
industry is a matter of interest.

August 31, 1934.
Mr. J. C. Goold,
Representative of Standard-Vacuum
Oil Company in Japan.
Dear Sir:
As per your letter dated Aug. 8, '34, you have
communicated with the Miniater of this Department with
reference to the enforcement of the Petroleum Industry
Law. The Government is not yet in a position to be
able to answer officially some of the questions stated
in your letter. However, we have decided to make you
a reply as follows. (For further particulars, you are
requested to call at this Department and have a talk with
tro officials concerned.):-
1. As per the provisions of the mupplementary
regulations of the Petroleum Industry Law, those who
are engaged in the business of importing petroleum
at the time of the enforeement of the law shall be
recognized as licensed under the law and the continua-
tion of business shall be sanctioned. There is no
restraint upon the period of the continuation of
business.
2. Annual quantity of petroloum to be sold by
each petroleum importer or petroleum refiner shall be
fixed in accordance with their business plans. Busimess
plans shall be sanetioned taking demand and supply of
petroleum and other things into consideration in order
to make our petroleum industry more prosperous. Henee
it is impossible to fix the quantity to be sold by oil
companies in advanee. The demand for petroleum is on
the imerease year by year in this country. Unless some-
thing extraordimary takes place, the quantity to be sold
will not become less than the quantity sold at the time
of the enforeement of the patroleum industry law.
3. Anmual sanction of busimess plans will not pre-
vent oil industrialists from carrying on business eom-
timuously. This annual sanetion will control demand and
supply of petroleum and prevent oil industrialists from
competimg with each other reeklessly. These Plans will
be used as data necessary in conneetion with supervision
ef the petroleum business. In this way, we think we will
be able to advanee the petroleum industry of this country.
Yours truly,
T. FUKUDA,
Ghief of the Mining mureem
of the Department of Gommeree
and Industry.

firms feel themselves now or about to be, in connection

glad to do so.

any aotion by the British. I told him that we had not;

Representations Made by Other Governments.
September 19, 11 a.m., the Netherlands Minister on
September 18, 1934, called on the Minister for Foreign
Affairs and left with him an aide memoire listing the
difficulties which the new petroleum regulations are
causing to the Netherlands oil interests and request-
ing the Japanese Government to consider means to
remedy the causes which render it difficult for the
Netherlands oil interests to arrive at a decision as to
their future course of action in Japan. The full text
1/
of this aide memoire 1s appended hereto. The Netherlands
oil interests referred to, it may be explained, are those
connected with the Royal Dutch-Shell interests, whose
subsidiary company in Japan is the Rising Sun Petroleum
Company. The bulk of the refined petroleum products
imported into Japan by this subsidiary company originates
in the Netherlands Indies, and consequently the
Netherlands Government is also interested in the subject
of oil control in Japan.
The Embassy has discovered that the British Ambassa-
dor to Japan, on July 26th last, made somewhat similar
representations to the Japanese Government. At that time
the foreign oil companies were primarily interested in
obtaining from the Japanese Government some inkling as
to their chances of being permitted to remain in business

=== Page 406 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
in Japan, and the representations made by the British
Ambassador were therefore principally concerned with
the problem of forcing answers to the oil companies
questions from the Ministry of Commerce and Industry.
A copy of the aide memoire which the British Ambassador
2/
left at the Foreign Office is enclosed herewith.
No replies have been received either by the British
Embassy or the Netherlands Legation to their represen-
tations, although the Commercial Counsellor of the
British Embassy was assured by the Chief of the Bureau
of Commercial Affairs of the Foreign Office that the
matter would receive the careful attention of the Foreign
Office.
Attitude of the Foreign Office toward the Question.
3/
There is enclosed herewith a copy of a memorandum
of a conversation which Mr. Neville had on September 13,
1934, with Mr. Kurusu, the Chief of the Commercial Bureau
of the Foreign Office. According to Mr. Kurusu's state-
ments, the Japanese Foreign Office is inclined to doubt
the feasibility of the stock-holding requirements of the
new law, because the Japanese oil companies lack the funds
necessary to erect storage facilities, and is inclined to
assist the foreign oil companies in their difficult posi-
tion. Mr. Kurusu is also well aware of the danger of
pushing the foreign companies too hard, and fears the
imposition of retaliatory measures by the British and
American

=== Page 407 === (confidence 0.99)
American Governments. In a previous conversation with
Mr. Dickover, Mr. Kurusu stated that he had heard rumors
of a possible embargo by the foreign oil companies or
their governments on the export of crude oil to Japan,
and further stated that such an embargo would be a
very serious matter to Japan, as the supply of crude oil
was not only an industrial necessity but involved the
question of national defense. The Foreign Office there-
fore seems to realize the implications in the situation
(which the Army and Navy apparently do not realize) and
is endeavoring to induce the other governmental depart-
ments concerned to relax their pressure on the foreign
oil companies.
Attitude of the Foreign Qil Companies toward the
Stock-Holding Provisions of the Law.
According to the regulations, the oil companies must
submit to the Department of Commerce and Industry, by
September 30, 1934, their business plans for the six
months' period beginning January 1, 1935. The plans
called for are to include the companies' projects for
erecting storage facilities, in compliance with the
stock-holding requirements of the law. The two foreign
oil companies are loath to submit these plans, as they
will then be obligated to carry them into execution.
On the other hand, failure to submit the plans will
comprise an infraction of the law and may lead to can-
celation of their operating licenses. The foreign oil
companies

=== Page 408 === (confidence 0.99)
-5-
companies are thus placed in a dilemma from which there
seems to be no escape at the moment. As was stated in
174
the Embassy's despatch No. 957, the Standard-Vacuum Oil
Company has definitely refused to erect storage facili-
ties unless the cost of the tanks and other equipment
is borne by the Japanese Government. According to the
Japanese press, Mr. H. W. Malcolm, the General Manager
of the Rising Sun Petroleum Company, who sailed for
London on September 15, 1934, to consult with the head
office officials of the Shell oil interests, stated
before he sailed that his Company could not obey the
regulations for the compulsory storage of oil in Japan.
On the other hand, according to Mr. Kurusu, the Chief
of the Commercial Bureau of the Foreign Office, the
Japanese oil companies are not financially able to
comply witn the oil storage provisions of the law. The
only foreign oil company operating in Japan which has
agreed to comply with the stock-holding provisions of
the law is the Associated Oil Company of California,
which operates in connection with the Mitsubishi interests.
It is presumed that the Mitsubishi interests are to bear
the bulk of the cost of the storage facilities of the
Associated Oil Company.
If the foreign and Japanese oil companies are
unable or refuse to comply with the oil storage regula-
tions, the Government could reduce their import and sales
guotas

=== Page 409 === (confidence 0.99)
-6-
quotas for the future to an appropriate proportion of
their existing storage facilities. This step would,
however, appear to be liable to create an acute shortage
of oil products in Japan, thus defeating the purposes of
the law. It seems, therefore, that it may become neces-
sary for the Government to abandon or modify its oil
storage requirements, at least for the present.
In the meantime the Japanese newspapers have been
conducting a campaign against the foreign oil companies
because of their refusal to comply with the oil storage
regulations. The TOKYO ASAHI on September 13 stated that
The (oil storage) reports have been submitted to the
authorities by the Japan Oil Company and other Japanese
concerns in general, but the two British and American
companies have not submitted any report at all to the
authorities". On September 17, the same newspaper
published an article stating, inter alia:
"As this has much to do with the defense of
our country, native oil companies are purchasing
sites necessary for stock-holding purposes and
are constructing tanks so that by October next
year the quantity to be held in stock may reach
the required standard. However, the Rising Sun
Petroleum Company and the Standard-Vacuum Oil
Company are not making preparations in this
line, saying that the Petroleum Industry Law
is the worst law in the world. These two for-
eign companies are not desirous of performing
the stock-holding duty. Hence the authorities
are worrying over the affair.*
The TOKYO NICHI-NICHI and the TOKYO JIJI also published
articles along the same line, indicating that the items
were obtained from the same source. This source is
presumed

=== Page 410 === (confidence 0.99)
-7-
presumed to be the Department of Commerce and Industry,
which is endeavoring to absolve itself from blame for
any controversy which may arise in the future and to throw
the onus upon the foreign oil companies.
Foreign Refineries in Japan.
The Standard-Vacuum Oil Company, as far as the
Embassy is aware, has not considered applying for a license
to erect a refinery in Japan, but the Rising Sun Petro-
leum Company has been exploring the possibilities, in
the hope of thereby avoiding some of the disabilities
under which the foreign companies labor. This company,
however, has not been able to ascertain if such a license
would be issued, or, if issued, whether or not the foreign
refinery would receive the same rights and privileges as
are or may be granted to Japanese refineries. On Septem-
ber 18, 1934, Rengo reported that the Department of
Commerce and Industry would grant refining licenses to
foreign oil companies, "there being no legitimate reason
for rejection, in so far as the Petroleum Industry Law is
concerned". The CHUGAI SHOGYO, however, reported on
September 18:
"Opinion is divided among Government author-
ities in the matter of sanctioning construction
of refineries by foreign concerns. Some of
them declare that it should be permitted in
spheres where the sales of Japanese companies
would not be affected, but others contend that
the Petroleum Industry Law was enacted with
the principal object of protecting Japanese
oil companies and that the foregoing sanction
would run against the spirit of the Law. Be-
cause of this disagreement, the matter will be
the

=== Page 411 === (confidence 0.98)
-8-
the subject of serious discussion at the meeting
of the Petroleum Committee to be held soon."
Restriction of Exports of Crude Qil to Japan and
Manchuria.
293
42
Reference is made to the Embassy's telegram No. 191,
August 31, 2 p.m., stating that the Standard interests,
the Shell interests and the Texas Oil Company (the three
foreign oil companies now doing business in Manchuria)
had agreed not to submit quotations for the supply of
crude oil to the refinery at Dairen of the Manchuria Oil
793.636271.11/213
Company, and to the Embassy's telegram No. l96, Septem-
ber 5, 1 p.m., stating that the above-mentioned agreement
was being rendered ineffective by the fact that the
Standard Oil Company of California and the Union Oil
Company of California were submitting bids for Kettleman
crude oil mixed with kerosene to the Manchuria Oil
Company. A few days later the Embassy learned that the
Standard Oil Company of California and the Union Oil
Company had withdrawn their bids, indicating that pressure
from some source unknown to the Embassy had been applied
in the United States to these two companies. The Embassy
has now learned, however, that another American oil com-
pany, the Rio Grande Oil Company, which formerly did no
business whatever in Manchuria, has sold ten thousand
tons of California crude oil to the Manchuria Oil Company.
Although

=== Page 412 === (confidence 0.99)
-9-
Although the problem of the Manchuria Oil Company
and the proposed oil monopoly in Manchuria is not direct-
ly connected with the question of the petroleum control
instituted in Japan, the above case is cited as an example
of the impossibility of establishing an effective control
of crude oil exports to Japan and Manchuria, as a retalia-
tory measure, without governmental action.
Rumor of Further Control in Japan.
The Embassy has been informed by the representative
in Japan of the Associated Oil Company that he learned
from his Japanese connection (Mitsubishi) that the Japanese
authorities intend to proceed gradually with their original
plan, which was to establish a central petroleum sales
organization which would monopolize the sales of petroleum
products in Japan. According to the report, this central
sales organization will soon be established, but at first
will handle only the products of the Japanese refineries,
leaving the foreign companies to continue to market their
owm products. Through the operation of the Petroleum
Industry Law, however, the quotas given the foreign com-
panies will gradually be reduced, until finally they will
be shut out of the market entirely and will be allowed
to sell only to the central sales organization. This
plan is to be adopted instead of an outright monopoly,
because, if a monopoly is established, the Japanese Govern-
ment will be morally obligated to take over the foreign
companies

=== Page 413 === (confidence 0.96)
-10-
companies' distributing properties at a fair valuation,
while, if the foreign companies are gradually "squeezed
out", the Government will not be under any obligation
to buy their properties.
Respectfully yours,
Sdani mecith.

AIDE MEMOIRE
The Bataafsche Petroleum Company which is
operating in Japan through the Rising Sun Comnany
has pointed out to the Netherlands Government the
very difficult position in which the Netherlands
oil interests in Japan are placed by the new re-
gulations of the Petroleum Industry Law of March
27, 1954 and the Imperial ordinances therewith con-
nected.
As has been presented on several occasions by
the "Rising Sun" Company to the Minister of Commerce
and Inaustry they consider several of the above men-
tioned regulations to be of a very detrimental nature
to their business and hard to comnly with. The most
outstanding of these are the following:
1. The requirement of holding a six months'
stock of various petroleum products in the interest
of the Japanese Government, which involves a con-
siderable outlay of capital, whilst no security
beyond one year is given for the required investments.
2. The system of annual licenses both for im-
porting and refining, in consequence of which no
security is given as to the volume of trade that may
be expected in the future.
3. The encouragement by the law of the refining
industry in Japan, a policy which is detrimental to the
refining industries which are carried on at the points
of

=== Page 415 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
of production of crude oil and with which Netherlands
interests are specially concerned. The Netherlands oil
interests state that notwithstanding the disadvantages
following the uneconomical effects of refining in the
consuming country instead of at the point of crude oil
production, they are willing to consider the erection
of a refining plant in Japan but so far they have not
been able to ascertain if a license to refine oil in
Japan will be granted them and if so whether they will
enjoy the same treatment as the Japanese companies.
4. The regulation that prices will be fixed by
the Government which can compel the oil comp nies to
sell their stocks and to continue to import ano sell
according to their import program, (which they have
to submit at fixed periods) at a price fixed by the
Government.
The Netherlands oil interests represent that they
are anxious to continue their long record of cooperation
in the petroleum business in Japan but in view of the
investments which they are now called upon to make under
the Petroleum Industries Law they feel it impossible
to come to a decision before knowing fully the con-
ditions under which they will be allowed to carry on
their future trade.
The Netherlands Government, which is closely
following the developments of the oil trade in Japan,
fully understands the difficult position in which the
Netherlands oil interests are placed and the apprehension
they

=== Page 416 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
they feel for their future trade in view of the
extensive powers conferred on the Government by the
Petroleum Industry Law of 1934. In consideration of
the very important Netherlands interests which are at
stake, the Netherlands Legation, acting under in-
structions of its Government, takes leave to anoroach
the Japanese Government with the recuest to consider
means to remedy the detrimental causes hich render
it extremcly difficult for the Netherlands oil in-
terests to re:ch a decision which is bound to in-
volve great expenditure for which at present no
sufficient security is iven.
September 18th.1934.

AIDE
MEMOIRE
CONFIDENTIAL
The British oil interests operating in Japan
have represented to His Majesty's Government that
they are placed in a di ficult nosition by the de-
velopment of the legislation for the control of the
petroleum industry in Japan.
They state that the objects of this legislation
are
1. The encouragement of the refining
industry in Janan
The holding of stocks of oil in Japan.
Oil companies are called upon to submit their sele
and import plans for the period July to December
by July 3lst next and on the basis of these plans
they have to accumulate and carry stocks by March
31st next.
They have therefore an extremely short period-
until July 3lst- in which to make an important decision
on policy and investment of capital. They must reach
a decision as to whether they will undertake large
expenditure on tanks for storage of oil, and also
as to whether they should build a refinery. They
represent that in both cases they are without in-
formation sufficient to reach a decision. Pending
the formation of an Advisory Committee under the new
Petroleum

=== Page 418 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
Petroleum Law they cannot tell whether they will
receive a license to refine oil in Japan. Similarly,
they are faced with the difficulty of deciding whether
the investment of a large sum in any form is justified
since the Law institutes a system of annual licenses
both for importing and r fining and therefore gives
them no security as to the volume of trade that may be
expectea in the future.
The British oil interests renresent that they are
anxious to continue their long record of cooperation in
the petroleum business in Janan. They feel willing
and able to cooperate in the carrying out of the pre-
sent policy; but they re now called upon under the Law
to make investments which may anount to several millions
of yen at short notice but without any degree of cer-
tainty as to the future on which they can justify a
great capital outlay.
The foregoing is the substance of the views taken
by the British oil interests concerned. His Majesty's
Embassy feel that these anorehensions are not without
serious foundation in view of the wide powers of inter-
vention conferred upon the cuthoritics by the Petro-
leum Industry Law of 1934. They take leave therefore to
suggest that in the interests not only of the British
firms but also of the petroleum industry as a whole the
Japanese Government should agree to a discussion with
the British oil importers of the position arising out
of the new Law, since, failing a clear interpretation
of

=== Page 419 === (confidence 0.98)
-3-
of the meaning of certain articles and exnlicit
assurances on certain points, it is hardly possible
for the British interests concerned to reach a decision
involving very great expenditure which might in the
future be rendered fruitless.
26th Ju1y, 1934.

=== Page 420 === (confidence 0.98)
C Q P Y
MEMORANDUM
Interview between Mr. Neville and Mr.
Kurusu, Chief of the Commercial Bureau
of the Foreign Office, September 13,
1934.
*************************
011.
Mr. Kurusu said that he did not think that the
matter of the Manchurian oil monopoly was imminent,
because the situation growing out of the vexed question
of Japanese administrative control would have to be
settled first. He said that he could not comment on
this, as it seemed to have got into a jam. So far as
the domestic situation was concerned, he said that the
Army and particularly the Navy were insisting upon all
sorts of ridiculous provisions in the Control Law
regulations. He said that the six months storage
requirement was practically impossible of enforcement,
because the oil companies did not have the money to put
up so many tanks. He said that he had told the other
Departments that the Government would either have to
assist the companies to carry such large stocks of oil
or else to permit them to raise their prices enough to
cover the carrying charge. He said that he had also
spoken to the other members of the oil control board
warning them that it would be poor policy to make the
oil business unprofitable to the foreign companies,
because if the latter were forced out of business,
the Japanese companies would find difficulty in getting
a supply of oil; that the United States might decide to
control oil exports and that the Japanese concerns
might then find themselves in a very embarrassing posi-
tion. He said that he was trying hard to get some kind
of an assurance for the foreign oil concerns, who could
not be expected to expend capital sums in Japan without
knowing whether they would be able to do business for
a definite period of years. He said he hoped he could
avoid making the matter an international question, with
the possibility of retaliatory measures by the Americans
and the British, who could make matters very troublesome
for Japan if they set about it.
水水水水水***水**水***********
E. L. N.

Japanese Petroleum Monopoly and
Related Developments and Problems.
This conversation was held in the office of the Under
Seoretary of State, from 3:30 to 4:45 this afternoon.
Mr. Teagle began with a statement, on the basis of a
memorandum (attached), on the subject of the Japanese
petroleum monopoly. Reference to the text of this memo-
randum shows more accurately than did his statement what
are the facts upon which he based and advanced the conten-
tion that it would be impossible from a business point of
view to comply with the provisions of the law and the
regulations appertaining to it, if enforced, and that the
LED
net effeot would be an enforoed withdrawal of the American
and British-Dutoh petroleum interests now operating in
Japan. At various points in his presentation of the matter
Mr. Teagle's statements were corroborated by statememts by
F/G
Sir Henri Deterding.
X
When
L

=== Page 428 === (confidence 0.98)
一8-
When Mr. Teagle reached the point of suggesting a
course of action, he suggested two things: that the American,
the British and the Netherland Governments make a joint pro-
test at Tokyo; and that the American Government make a gesture
by having an inquiry addressed by the Secretary of the
Interior to American petroleum interests calling for a
canvass of the situation with a view to possible measures
of restriction upon exports (to Japan). When asked which
of these measures he sought to have taken first, Mr. Teagle
seemed to be of the view that they might be taken simul-
taneously, but he seemed particularly intent upon the idea
that the American Government should act and that action
taken by it in addressing the American oil interests could
be taken expeditiously and if taken would have the immediate
effect of frightening the Japanese and perhaps leading
promptly to a revision by the Japanese of their position.
Mr. Hornbeck and Mr. Phillips asked a number of ques-
tions direoted toward eliciting information with regard to
the position and authority of the British and the Dutch
Governments in regard to measures which might be taken
toward ensuring effeotiveness of action by the oil industries
in support of protests, if and when made, by governments.
At no time in the course of the conversation was there
given any indioation that the British or the Dutoh Govern-
ments had committed themselves to a course of action either
of protest or of restriction.
At

=== Page 429 === (confidence 0.97)
- 3 -
At an appropriate moment Mr. Phillips read the text
of Tokyo's telegram No. 182, August 20, 1934, 3 p.m. The
suggestion made in that telegram that a gesture be made by
the American Government is similar to the suggestion made
by Mr. Teagle in the early stages of the conversation.
After some further disoussion, Mr. Phillips inquired
whether Mr. Teagle and Sir Henri would like to talk with
the Seoretary of the Interior; and in consequence of this
suggestion and replies in the affirmative, Mr. Phillips
made by 'phone appointments for a conference to be held
at the office of the Secretary of the Interior on Thursday,
August 23, at ll:10 a.m., the Secretary of the Interior to
receive Mr. Hornbeck first and ten minutes later to receive
Mr. Teagle and Sir Henri.
Mr. Phillips then raised the question as to what should
be said to the press if inquiries were made by the correspond-
ents with regard to the conversation which was just closing.
It was agreed that Mr. Teagle and Sir Henri should refer any
inquirers to Mr. Phillips and that Mr. Phillips should say
that the subjeot of the conferenoe had been a question of
the quality of commercial opportunity in the Far East.
Mr. Teagle and Sir Henri thanked Mr. Phillips for having
received them and given them a good deal of time and, with
the usual amenities, the conversation ended.

August l933, about the time Matsukata competition appeared, the Cartel
was renewed and the Shell's and our adherence to it, was made subject to
the Government controlling Russian prices and assigning them a reason-
able quota, but unfortunately, the Government did not act to any pur-
pose until June 1934, when a temporary quota was given to the Russians
and prices were partially restored but not sufficiently to cover our
oosts.
Government control has just recently culminated in the Petroleum
Industry Control Law, which has placed more onerous conditions on the
industry than ever attempted in any other country on any industry. This
Law was enacted on March 27, 1934, promulgated June 27th, and became
offeotive July lst, l934. It gives the Government full regulation of
prices to any extent they deem fit. All concerns proposing to carry on
a business of refining petroleum or of importing petroleum, must obtain

=== Page 431 === (confidence 0.98)
-2
permit and formulate a business plan whioh must have the approval of the Govern-
memt. Any ohamge in the plan or proposal to transfer, abolish or suspend the whele
or part of the undertaking under the plan., must have the sanotion of the Government.
Ome of the most onerous conditions imposed is that all refiners and importers of
petroloum shall keep continuously in stook a quantity of petroleum caloulated on
the basis of the quantity which they formerly imported. It neoessitates the fil-
ing, prior to September 30, 1934, of the complete import and sales plan for 1935
and the filing at onco of a similar plan for the last half of l934. The rogula-
tions state that by April lst, 1935, we must aocumulato stooks in bond, roughly
•quivalent to 3 months business, and to be considered purely roserve stooks and
in adaition to stooks n rmally necessary for ordinary business. By October 1935
this reserve must be inoreased to á months stock. Regardless of vory ample tankage
we have in Japan it will mean an oxpenditure of approximately U. S. $375,000.
to make our storage oomply, and we understand the Shell interests will have to
spend about . S. $2,700,O00. Our investment in stooks unnecessarily tied up
would be approximatoly U. S. $1,900,000. A very important point is that the
Government have full control over current sales prices and in an emergoncy have
the privilege of taking over these reserve stooke at the current price in Japan
regardless of whether this prioe is below our laid-down costs in Japan or below
the basis of world prices. After taking over our stook, to continue in business
in Japan we womld then be obliged to roplenish this stook, which in turn could
again be taken over by the Government, and so on indofinitely.
The Control Law also states that plans for rofining or for
inorease in refining prodution .ust be declared by Septouber 30th, and the plans
embrace the necessity of giving considerable detail as to capacity, type of re-
fining units and total expenditures, While no figures which wo have been able
to assemble show that it will be profitable for us to croot a refinery in Japan,
nevertholess it is not clear to us whether by failure to submit a plan wo would
be eliminated from later rofining, or whether, if we did apply wo would be ao
tually oommitted to the full extont of our plan or to tho plan in a modified way.
The Japanese Governmont have progressively inoreased Finished Produot duties
in favor of refining orude, and it is concoivable that they might so inorease

We have cabled Yokohama that at the present time we cannot
compile figures which would show any profit in a refinery and are convinoed that
we would not prepare to creot at this time and that this decision is qualified
only should it exolude us from any future opportunity to build a refinery unless
we file applioation promptly. Wo advised thom also that in respeot to building
of 3 and 6 months storage we are willing to comply if the Govonnment furnishes
money to provide tankage and pay for exoess stock as well as to indemnify us
against evaporation losses, including intorest or any other additional expense,
and failing this we are certainly unaltorably opposed to a huge additional
utlay, partioularly in view of oontinued unprofitable prices whioh under

in the situation to the Stato Department.

TIOLE FRON THE SOVIET NEWBPAPER "PRAVDA", DATED 13th JULY, 1934.
JAPANOS TURL BASE
The fuel problem is one of the most acute problems oonfronting Japanese imper-
1aliem. In respect of natural reserves of fuel Japan has at her disposal 2.5 peroent
of the world's potential resources of hydro-energy, 2.3 percent of the oil, O.4 per-
cent of the lignine, and O.l percent of the coal. Japanese imperialiam has tried to
compensate itself by means of the forced working of its existing resources, and the
seisure of colonial sources of raw materials. If one also makes a reduction on ac-
count of the climate, which lowers the consumption of fuel, all these figures show,
however, that with the exception of hydro-energy Japan is far behind her imperialist
colleagues as far as fuel is concerned.
However, with all the forcing of the use of "white coul", this cannot take the
place of other kinds of fuel. There is no peat in Japn, likewise very little lignine,
although in the fomm of chercoal it is a universal domestic 1el, und in 1931 50,118
thousand cob. wetres timber were hewn for this purpose, as compared with 13, 597 thous.
cub. metres for business prpses. In uddi tion to these kirds of fuel there re ul so
coal und oil.
The potertial reserves of cou! of Ju un herself are reckoned at anproximately
6.950 million tons, but only 900 million tons re of any industrial importunce. Coal
production ettained its meximm roportions in 1929 - 34, 58 thous. tons, but fell to
27,987 thous. tons in 193l. The general consumption of ccl is on an verage about
30,ooo,ooo tons. un the hole up to the resent Japan has reuined a "self-supporting"
nation as far us coal is concerned.
The Fleet consumes 1 percent of the totul ccal output, the railways - 10 percent.
metallurey - 10 percent, Eus production - 4 percent, electric power stations - 3 percent
and the munufacturing industries - 48 percent. whereas durine the last few pre-
crisis years e number of countries, vith neland at the ir heed, produced less coal then
in pre-war times, Japan increased her production by more than 40 percent as compared
with l9l3. However, although in England the price of & ton of coal hus actually

=== Page 438 === (confidence 0.98)
-2-
manged to a very slight extent, in Japan coal, even in l930, cost twice as much as
in pre-war times. Hence a strong tendenc yto change over from coal to other kinds of
fuel, and in particular to oil and electricity.
Oil consumption is growing at a rapid rate. Apart from the Navy, in l913
Japan consumed approximately 350,000 tons of oil, while in 1930 consumption rose
to 1.5 million tons. Duri ng the cricis year of 193o the consumption of oil in-
creased by 6 percent, while during  in's most difficult crisis year - l931 - it
further increased by 15 percent. The structurel alterutions which took place
during this period wth recurd to the consunption of oil pro ducts are charucterized
by an acute fall in the consumption of kerosene und asoline und a very lurge abso-
lute and also comparative growth in the consumption of mazout and benzine. The
rapid growth of motor traffic and aviation, and likewise the considerable fall in
the price of benzine explain the gmwth in consumption of same, while the mass
conversion of fishing vessels to liquid fuel and the accumulation of strategical
reserves provided the reason for the £rowth in the denand for macout.
However, with such a tempestuous growth in consumption the production of oil
in Japen has not increased: in 1913 it umount to 223,000 tons, and, reaching 1ts
maximm height in 1916, is now steadily falling. In 1932 42,000 tons were pro-
duced, but although in 1913 this came to 60 percent of the country's consumption,
in l933 the specific grevity of its own oil fell to l0percent. In addition to
this, the old and principel oil regions are becoming exhausted: the Niigat and
Akita regions, which yl elded more than 0 percent of the total Japanese production
during the decade 1919 - l929, hive reduced the production of oi1 by 21 percent.
The government have taken every possible step to increase production: they
introduced a very high tariff an all imported oil products, and since l926 subsidies
have been paid to persons conduc ting explorations in new areas. However, this has
not helped: production has not increased, and the number of explored locations during
the period between the end of the imperialist war and the crisis was reduced by 34
porcent, and the number of locations under drilling by l6 percent. The only fields

=== Page 439 === (confidence 0.98)
-3-
whieh have yielded a slightly higher production since the war are the Isikari,
Tiosio, Kitami and Ibouri fields, all on the island of Hokkaido, which play a
vory amall role in Jepanese production.
The acute decrease in production at the large fields constitutes the best
proof of the exhaustion of the oilfields: although in l9l9 there were several
oilfields with an annual pmduction of more than 14 million gallons, by the time
the world crisis arose there was not one; however, the number of fields increased,
with an output from 2.5 million to 5 mdllion gallons. The average daily production
of one well in Junan amounts to only one-fifth of the everage daily production of
one well in the U.s.A.
Having utilized all the possibilities within the country, and not having
achieved any practical results worth mentioning, Japanese iaperialism has started
exploration operations in its coloni es. In Kore no oil has heen found so far, however
in Formosa exploration has yielded favorable results: in 1919 1,174 tons were pro-
duced, and in 1928 as ma ny as anprox. 44.00 tons. lowever, since 198 production
has also decreased to a considereble extent in Formosa; in l929 it amounted to
33.000 tons, and in 1930 - 32.000 tons.
Some foreign experts consider that with the object of ensuring suoplies in the
ovent of war a certain number of the wells is being conserved. It is of course
possible to make such reserves, but in ny case they cannot be of eny serious im-
portance, as the conservation of even z0 percent of possible production would yield
an increase in production of only 50 - 60 thousand tons per nnum, which constitutes
only 2 percent of the annual consumption of the Japanese navy, air force and army in
wartime.
O11 imports into Japan continue to grow from one year to another. In 1913 they
mounted to 197,000 tons, not including the direct imports of the inistry of arine,
in 1925 - 586,000 tons, and in 1933 - approximately 2.5 mil1ion tons. In the May
number (1934) of the journal "Kogio Nikhon" Vice-Admiral Nakasato, who is the chair-
man of the "Kita Karafuto Sekiu" wrote: "Japan is suffering from a shortage of oil.

=== Page 440 === (confidence 0.97)
he all problee is a very diffioult one for Jepan. This question became pertieulegly
ute after the events in Hanohuria, and is now still more soute in viow of the
atheemins difficul tues in 1935 - 1936. Therefore the solution of this problem
1ill brook no dolg."
In mhat direction is Japanese iperialism turaing for the solution of this
problem? The production of the North Sakhalin concession amounted to 13.770 tong
1n 1925, in 1930 - 193,000 tons, and in 1933 it almost reached the level of pro-
duction of Japan, mounting to 220,000 tons.
In addition to the North Sakhalin concession Japan likewise has concessions
on the island of Borneo (Indonesia) and in Texas (in exico). The Japanese press
is silent concerning the progress of the operations at the latter; with regard to
the former, in the spring of 1930 43 engineers and 25 technicians were sent to
Tarakan, and have developed the work consi derably. However, the practical value
of the Borneo concession is so far inconsiderable, as it is situated in the mount-
ains, and the oil produced has to be carried away in barrels on mule-back. Com-
munications in the foretan press show that at the prssent time the Jaranese are
engaged in Eornen ct only, or rtr not so much in the froduction of oil as in
tudying the shores of the island and arranging for open spaces for future aerodromes!
During the past few years Japanese i perialism has been zealously searching for
oil in China. In buron Tunaka's memorundum the importance of anchurian oil was men-
tioned. although there are no offielal data regarding oil deposits, nevertheless the
1dea is widespread that in Jehol, in Northem China, oil exis ts, and thut secret
oxploration operations have ylel ded positive results.
If we add together the production of Japan herself, her colonies and concessions,
and also add the 150,000 tons of Soviet sukhalin oil obtained, this quantity does
not oover one third of Japan's total requirements of oil. If from this quantity
we deduct the production of Sakhalin, an "impossible situation" - to use the words
of Nakasato - is created. True, in "peace-tine" this does not cause Japanese
imperialism any real difficulty.

Subject:
Japanese Petrcleum Monopoly and
Related Developments and Problems.
Control by the Japanese Government
Over the Japanese Cil Industry; the
FILED
Effects thereof on American and British
Oil Enterprises in Japan; Suggested
1930
Remedial Action.
Reference: Memoranda of August 22 and 23, 1934.
Mr. Hornbeck and Mr. Dooman called by appointment at
eleven o'clock this morning at the office of the Secretary
of the Interior and there found waiting Sir Henri Deterding.
Mr. Teagle arrived a few minutes later. By previous
Confidential File
arrangement Mr. Hornbeck conferred privately with Mr. Ickes
for a few minutes, and at the end of that conference
Sir Henri, Mr. Teagle and Mr. Dooman were ushered into
Mr. Ickes' office.
Mr. Teagle
G

=== Page 449 === (confidence 0.99)
-2 -
Mr. Teagle began the conversation by stating that his
company and Sir Henri's company were in serious trouble.
Mr. Ickes remarked that the trouble must indeed be serious
1f Mr. Teagle and Sir Henri both and jointly sought his
(Mr. Ickes') help. Mr. Teagle replied that such indeed
was the case, as both his company and that of Sir Henri
had concluded that it would be impossible to carry on
operations in Japan under the new Japanese law for the
control of the oil industry. Mr. Teagle explained that
about two years ago there was instituted in Japan a system
similar to that of the N.R.A. in the United States whereby
about a year ago the oil industry among others was placed
under control of the Japanese Government. Prices of oil
products were fixed without regard to world parity of
prices and when a Japanese merchant imported under contract
with the Soviet Government ten million gallons of gasoline,
the American and British companies were directed by the
Japanese Government to make room for the sale of the con-
signment from the Soviet Union. When the foreign companies
sought to raise the price of gasoline to a point somewhere
approaching the world parity of price, the Japanese Govern-
ment withheld its necessary approval on the ground that the
taxicab-drivers objected to the increase in price. This
year a law was enacted for the control of the oil industry.
Under this law it is provided that the foreign companies
must

=== Page 450 === (confidence 0.99)
-3 -
must keep in stock in Japan one-half the quantity 1mported
by them during the previous year, such stock may at any
time be taken over by the Japanese Government at the local
market price, a price fixed by itself, and in such event
the company must immediately replendish the stock. Under
the law the Japanese Government could repeat this process
indefinitely. It is impossible, continued Mr. Teagle,
for his company and that of Sir Henri to expect to operate
profitably in Japan under such conditions. When making the
foregoing statement, Mr. Teagle repeatedly turned to
Sir Henri for corroboration, which corroboration Sir Henri
gave.
Mr. Ickes then asked how he "fitted into the picture".
Mr. Teagle replied that the total production in Japan of
oil amounted to approximately 15 per cent of Japan's
requirements; that Japan is dependent upon supplies of oil
from the United States principally and from Netherlands
possessions; that the Mitsubishi Refinery, in which there
is some investment of American capital, obtains its crude
oil from the Associated concern in California, and that
another Japanese refinery also obtains its crude oil from
California producers. Mr. Teagle and Sir Henri agreed that
probably something like 70 per cent of Japan's imports of
crude oil are purchased in the United States. Mr. Teagle
added that if an embargo were placed upon the exportation
of o11

=== Page 451 === (confidence 0.99)
- 4 -
of oil to Japan from California and Netherlands possessions,
Japanese vessels would become immobilized.
Mr. Ickes asked how the British and American companies
operating in Japan compared in point of business which they
did in Japan. Mr. Teagle replied that Sir Henri's company
did more business than did the Socony-Vacuum Company.
taken by Mr. Stimson against Japan and that Mr. Stimson did
not receive much support from other countries. It seemed
to him that in view of the relatively greater importance
of Sir Henri's company it might be appropriate for the
British Government to initiate action. However, he added,
that appeared to him to be a matter which is of primary
concern to the Department of State.
Sir Henri said that he had already approached the
Netherlands and British Governments in regard to the
possibilities of placing restrictions upon exportations of
oil to Japan and that those Governments were favorably and
sympathetically disposed toward the idea; but that neither
wished to be "the bad boy" in the case. He felt certain,
however, that those two Governments would be willing to
cooperate in any such project as that which Mr. Teagle had
suggested.
Mr. Ickes then asked Mr. Teagle what definite suggestion
he could make in the way of action by the Department of the
Interior. Mr. Teagle said that in the event of the refusal
of the

=== Page 452 === (confidence 0.98)
-5 -
to
of the big oil producers in the United States whiek sell
crude oil to the Japanese, the Japanese might suoeed in
making contracts with the smaller producers. It would be
necessary, therefore, that there be concerted action if any
definite results were to be achieved. It was his suggestion
that the Department of State write a letter to the Depart-
ment of the Interior, requesting that a survey be made of
the stocks of oil available for export and inquiring what
arrangements could be made for the restriction of exports
of oil from the United States; that thereupon the Department
of the Interior should cause the necessary survey to be
made, and through an appropriate agency, ascertain the possi-
bility of there being effected among the producers an agree-
ment to restrict exports of oil to Japan. Mr. Ickes remarked
that the suggestion seems to be a practical one, and he be-
lieved that even if some of the smaller producers "held out"
measures could be found to bring them into line.
Mr. Ickes asked Mr. Hornbeck whether he would indicate
the position taken by the Department of State in regard to
the project outlined by Mr. Teagle. Mr. Hornbeck said that
the Department had given the matter careful consideration
and that it felt that insofar as governmental action was con-
cerned the initiative should be taken by the British and/or
the Netherland Governments. He said that the Department had
mado

=== Page 453 === (confidence 0.99)
- 6-
made note of the faot that the British and Netherland oil
interests in Japan are greater than are those of the United
States and it therefore would seem that their Governments
should originate suggestions, if such suggestions were to
be made, for common action. Sir Henri and Mr. Teagle
demurred at the suggestion that this matter is of greater
concern to the British and to the Dutch than to American
interests, stating that the greater part of the oil imported
into Japan is purchased from American sources. Mr. Hornbeck
pointed out, however, that this fact would not appear to be
germane, the point being that the British and Dutch interests
directly to be affected by the operation of the Japanese law
(i.e., in business in Japan) are greater than the American
interests to be affected, and that the British and the Dutch
interests stand to lose more by the operation of the Japa-
nese regulations than do the American interests. Mr. Teagle
and Sir Henri assented. Mr. Hornbeck said that this being
the case the Department would not be dispósed to initiate a
proposal for joint action looking toward restricting the sale
of crude oil to Japan, but that if Sir Henri, upon his return
to Europe, approached the British and the Netherland Govern-
ments,Sir Henri might say to those Governments that if they
felt disposed to take the initiative they might expect to
find the American Government responsive toward the idea of
cooperation.
The conference with Mr. Ickes there ended.
Lator,

=== Page 454 === (confidence 0.96)
-7 -
Later, Sir Henri stated that he would leave the
United States on Saturday, that he would be in London not
later than on Friday next, and that he felt reasonably
certain that he would be able to induce the British Govern-
ment to make a definite move. He honed to be able to tele-
graph Mr. Teagle by Monday, September 2nd. Mr. Hornbeck
pointed out that insofar as governmental action is con-
cerned the approach to this Government should be by the
British and Dutch Governments. There followed discussion
of the place at which the approach should be made, and
there was reached a consensus of opinion that London would
probably be the most convenient place. Mr. Hornbeck
suggested that the approach should be made through the
instrumentality of a written communication, and there was
general consensus in that thought.

Subjeot:
Japanese Petroleum Monopoly and

Mr. Teagle called on me by appointment at 9:30 this
morning. In anticipation of this conversation, I had
conferred with the Under Secretary on recent developments
in this case and possible line of procedure.
After exchange of amenities, I informed Mr. Teagle
that we had received yesterday from London a telegram
which indicated that the British Foreign Office had the
mattor under consideration but that they had so far made
no follow-up with us on the exohange of messages which
VILED
took place upon their initiative nearly two weeks ago.
I said that in reply to their inquiry with regard to our
attitude we had informed them through our Embassy in
London that we felt that the matter was serious and that
we would be prepared to give sympathetic consideration to
F
suggestions which they might offer for action; that they
had
G

=== Page 461 === (confidence 0.98)
- 2 -
had remarked that this message was satisfactory "as far
as it went" but that they thought it would be difficult
to bring independent American companies in line; and that
we had had no word from them since.
At this point, and with Mr. Teagle's approval, I
aldsed Mr. Dooman and Mr. Hamilton to join us.
Mr. Teagle handed me a copy of a telegram which he
had received from Sir Henri Deterding. (A copy of this
telegram is attached to this memorandum.) After reading
aloud the telegram, I observed to Mr. Teagle that it is
misleading in respect of two statements: one, that the
Netherland Government had asked our "attitude toward
simultaneous protest", and, second, that "Washington
had not yet definitely agreed to protest". I said that
the Netherland Legation in Washington had informed us
to the effect, first, that the Netherland Government
was prepared in principle to protest and wished to know
our attitude, and subsequently that the Netherland
Minister at Tokyo had been instructed by his Government
to make representations to the Japanese Government. I
stated that we had received under date August 29 an inquiry
from the British Government in regard to the attitude of
this Government in the matter of the oil situation in
Japan, and that the reply which we made to the British
Government on the following day expressed readiness to
givo

=== Page 462 === (confidence 0.99)
-3 -
give sympathetio consideration to any proposal which the
British and Netherland Governments may wish to make to
us toward joint or concurrent action; but that we have
heard nothing further from the British Government: that we
are now in the position of waiting to receive some definite
proposal. I stated further that at no time had we received
any definite suggestion either from the British or the Dutch
in regard to the making of simultaneous representations.
Mr. Teagle stated that Sir Henri Deterding had
apparently been acting on the basis of an assumption that
it had been understood at the informal conference held on
August 23 in the office of the Secretary of the Interior
that the American Government would be prepared to give con-
sideration to a proposal to be initiated by the Netherland
Government (only). I remarked that I thought it had been
clearly understood by Sir Henri and Mr. Teagle and Mr. Dooman
and myself that the American Government would expect to
be approached by the British Government, and that Sir Henri
had stated immediately after that conference that he would
arrive in London on August 31 and would at once confer
with officials in the British Foreign Office.
Mr. Teagle said that, in view of the fact that the
question of action being taken in behalf of the foreign
oil companies in Japan is urgent, he thought it advisable
to correct Sir Henri's misapprehensions; that he would
telephone to Sir Henri's representative in New York,
requesting
